1990. EDWARD BISHOP , feloniously assaulting Jane Franklin, and stealing from her person, and against her will, 2 shillings, 3 pence, and 5 halfpence; her property.
JANE FRANKLIN . I am an unfortunate girl, and live in St. Ann-street. Last Saturday night week, at half-past twelve o'clock, I was going down Tothill-street—I went into the potato-shop, and got a halfpenny potato—I came out, the prisoner followed me, and as soon as I turned the corner no
knocked me down, and kicked me in the head, which I have a place to show—he tore my frock, and took 2 s. 5 1/2 d. from my pocket—I had taken my money out at the potatoe-shop, and changed a sixpence—I laid hold of the prisoner to keep him, and his companions rescued him from me—there were five or six men and some girls—I had known him before—by walking about the street.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long did you live with M'Coy? A. Idid not live with him at all; I lived in the same house with him—I did not live on the produce of the robberies he had committed—he was concerned in the matter of Mr. Bellchambers—I ate with him—we all eat together—we all put the same money together—I never had any money of him—there were four of us, Thomas Sale and his wife, and M'Coy and I.
JOHN FLYNN . On last Saturday night week I saw Jane Franklin on the pavement in Westminster very drank—I advised her to go home—she told me to go away, and she pushed me.
MARY M'CABE . I heard Jane Franklin say on the 21st Aug., so help her God, this was not the young man who took her money—she said she would be very sorry to prosecute him, and only for the policeman there would be no prosecution.
Cross-examined. Q. Did she say anybody struck her? A. Yes; the prisoner struck her, but had not robbed her.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18480821-1991]
1991. FREDERICK AUGUSTINE MOORE , feloniously threatening to accuse Thomas Phillips of making a solicitation to one John Moore, &c, and thereby extorting from him 5 sovereigns, his moneys.—Other Counts, for a robbery, and for demanding money with menaces.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS PHILLIPS . I am out of business—I am married, and am sixtyfive years old—I saw the prisoner about two years ago, and from time to time till 5th July, 1848—on that day I was returning to my house, on the east side of Bethnal-green, about three o'clock in the afternoon—I got out of an omnibus—the prisoner followed me, tapped me on the shoulder, and wished to speak to me—I told him I would have nothing to do with him—I immediately went home—in an hour or two afterwards a note was brought to me in pencil—it stated that he wished to see me about three o'clock, before the church on the Green; and then I received a second note, wishing to know whether I would come out, or he would call next day with a friend—the next day I saw him in the road, on returning home—he was alone—I asked what he was waiting for—he said he would tell me what he was waiting for, when his brother came—his brother had frequently made an accusation against me, and the prisoner has threatened me that he had it id his power to accuse me of improper conduct towards his brother—he said it was exposing myself to his brother John—I saw the prisoner again about three or four o'clock in the afternoon, with his brother John, on the north side of Bethnal-green—he then said he would have 10 l. and nothing less, as he was going to Australia, and if he had not that money, he had stones in his pocket, and he would break my windows—that he was so determined to go to Australia, that if I would give him 10 l. he would never annoy me again, and his brother would sign any document that I chose to draw up—we then agreed that I should go home and draw up this document, and meet them at the Greyhound with this document—I went home and wrote the document—this is it ( produced)—Rickman and Frederick signed it—I gave them 10 l.—I offered 5 l. at first, and Rickman said he would have the other five, and I crave 10 l., which Rickman
took—it was five sovereigns and a 5 l. note—on former occasions the prisoner had threatened me that if I did not give money they would charge me with exposing my person, and it was under that threat that I gave the money—there is not any truth in my having exposed my person to either—this indorsement was written by Rickman, for protection for himself he required me to sign it in the presence of the prisoner, and I did sign it—( read) "10 l. I hereby acknowledge that I have received, this 6th day of July 1848, 10 l.; and have also heretofore received various sums of Mr. Thomas Phillips, of Bethnal-green, and declare and solemnly avow, that I will never again call upon, annoy, or extort from the said Mr. Thomas Phillips, any money again, upon any pretence whatever, and disavow altogether all accusations that I have made injurious to his character, and in presence of my brother Rickman, M. Moore, and Mr. Thomas Phillips, declare this my true and solemn engagement. Witness my hand, this 6th day of July, 1848, also my brother, F. A. Moore, R. M. Moore." The indorsement on the back was—"6th July, 1848. I, Mr. Thomas Phillips, of Bethnal-green, do hereby declare that the former part is not in any way to produce information against F. or R. M. Moore. Thomas Phillips. Witness, R. M. Moore, F. Moore."
Prisoner. Q. Had you received any letter from my brother previous to seeing him? A. Yes, stating that he would call on me on the following morning; I received it in the forenoon; I went to his house in Goswell-street; I saw his wife; I do not know what I told her, I asked if I could see him; I was told he was not at home; I saw his wife in the street; I went to inquire after her; I did not tell her I had received some letters from her husband; I said about some papers, which I gave up to your brother, respecting the money that you and he had received, and he took the copy of them, and those he kept, and you said you would give them up; I gave him those papers, to show that you had received money; I did not own my guilt to him; I saw no more of you till the 5th of July; I do not know where you lived at the time these papers were given up.
Q. Do not you know I lived half a dozen doors off you, and did you ever pass there at ten o'clock at night, and stop till my brother John came out to you, and give him a shilling? A. I did once give him a shilling, when he said he was out of place; I did not go anywhere with him that evening; I did not go with him up Sugar-loaf-walk; I was in the habit of taking warm baths at the time your father was proprietor of the warm baths; your brother John attended me; I never acted in an indecent way towards him; I do not recollect that there was a hole bored in the wainscot, and that I asked what it was for; I do not recollect that I hung my watch before that hole; I do not recollect that I ever came in the back-way; I did not tell him the day that I should come there; I have been in the Angel and Crown public-house, which is next door to the baths, and he has come there to me; I did not tell him to come there to me; I have been in the public water-closet belonging to the Wesleyan Chapel, but not with him; one day he watched me, and followed me in and asked me for money; you threatened you would charge me with improper conduct unless I gave you 2 l.; we called on your brother John, you asked him if he had seen me; you signed this paper now produced, to assure me that you were going to Australia, and your brother should join in the signature that I should never be annoyed again; promising never again to come near me; it was on the 6th of July you received the money, and it was on the 14th of July you were given in charge.
COURT. Q. Did anything occur after this had been signed that induced you to give him in charge? A. Yes; on the 14th he called again, and Mrs.
Phillips saw him, and said we should have nothing to say to him, and he went away; she refused to have any communication with him; he came again with his brother Rickman; my wife went to the gate to him; he went away, and came again in an hour afterwards.
Prisoner. Q. Before I signed this paper you did not say that one reason you wished me to sign it was, to make sure that I would not mention the circumstance? A. No; you did not tell your brother to give me the money back; you snatched the paper out of your brother's hand, and returned again with it; after your brother had got the money he took away the paper; your brother offered to give me the money back; you did not offer to give me the paper back; not a word of it.
ALEXANDER STEWART ( policeman, 338 K.) In consequence of information from the prosecutor I took the prisoner into custody on this charge on the 15th July—he was then at Worship-street—he had been brought there on a previous charge—he said he should like to be taken into custody, Mr. Phillips knew all about it—I was present at his examination—I heard the Magistrate ask him if he wished to say anything—the clerk took down what he said—he signed his name to it—it was read over to him—this is his signature to it—( read —"The prisoner says, 'I have nothing more to say to convict me more than what has been said to convict me; I never extorted any money from him, nor did I ever threaten him; my first place of meeting Mr. Phillips was in the Hackney-road; I then mentioned to him the circumstance of my younger brother being in a situation in the Hackney-road; I told him that my brother had given me to understand that he, Mr. Phillips, had acted in a very indecent Way towards my brother;' Mr. Phillips said, 'I have known you for some years, for God's sake don't say anything about it;' I said, 'Well, Mr. Phillips, I have no wish to injure you in any way, no more than if you will not interfere with my younger brother any more, as you must be well aware the manner in which you have acted towards him is of a most filthy description;' he then asked me if I was doing anything; I said, 'No, I was out of a situation;' he said 'Meet me to-morrow at the Queen's Head, and I will talk to you upon the subject;' I met him there, and he asked me what I would have to drink; I told him, whatever he was going to drink I would drink with him; he then said, 'Mr. Moore, as you are out of a situation, these two pounds I give to you may be of service to you;' he gave me 2 l., but I in no way extorted them from him. Before I left him he said that at any time I met him and wanted money, and it was in his power, I should have it; I then told him, provided he did not annoy my brother any more. I never sent the two letters in pencil to him; I never asked him for the 10 l.; I was at the Salmon and Ball public-house at the time he got out of the omnibus; I came out and walked behind him, intending to go across the Park; as I was passing his house he stood at the parlour-window, he beckoned to me—he came out at his gate, went up the Green, then turned round and beckoned me again; he then said, 'If I let you have 10 l., will you sign a paper?' I said, 'Yes, I would.' I have nothing more to say at present."
GUILTY . Aged 21. Transported for Fifteen Years .
[trialAccount t18480821-1992]
1992. JAMES WALDEN , feloniously cutting and wounding Henry Allen, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. THOMPSON conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY ALLEN . On 18th July I was drinking with the prisoner the greater part of the day—about six o'clock that evening I saw him standing against the post outside the Pack Horse at Turnham-green—we had some words together, and then we walked into the house—I called for a quartern
of gin at the bar—there were three more persons beside the prisoner and I standing at the door—after I had called for the gin, the prisoner and I had a few words, and we began fighting—he said, "Recollect, if you and I fight this night, you will recollect it to the longest day you have to live"—I saw a knife in his hand, and in the scuffle I felt something at the back part of my thigh, which I thought was a kick—I pulled my trowsers open, and found a wound about four inches—he said, "Take that, you b----"—I was surrounded by his companions, and I began to fight as hard as I could for my liberty and when I found I could not get away I fell down—I was taken to St. George's Hospital, and the surgeon saw me that night—I was there three weeks—I am an out-patient now.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you believe this was done intentionally? A. Yes, I should say it was—I should not like to swear that he was the man that actually did it—he fell down, and he clasped my leg in falling down—it does not look much like an accident—it appeared that it was a stab first, and as I pulled him up, it cut—I had been drinking—I began at the Magpie and Stump, at Brentford, about eleven o'clock, and finished at Mitchell's, at the Salutation—Shirley came in at the Magpie and Stump—he was not drunk then—he got drunk afterwards—I carried him on my back—I am a hard-working man at anything I can get to do; not at prize-fighting, I do not like that game—I had one bit of a turn up five or six months ago—Spruce lives with me; I keep her.
SARAH SPRUCE . I was at the Pack Horse about half-past nine o'clock in the evening on 18th July—I saw the prisoner and the prosecutor quarrelling concerning a fight—they went into the house, I went to the door—it was open—I saw them begin to fight—the prisoner had a knife in his hand—the prisoner said, "If so be you and I fight, you will remember it the longest day you have to live"—they then commenced fighting.
Cross-examined. Q. What sort of a knife was this? A. Such as a man would carry in his pocket to eat his dinner—I cannot tell who hit the first blow—one blow followed the other as near as they could—the prosecutor is not quarrelsome if he is not upset—I live with him.
JOHN WARDLE ( policeman, T 219). I saw the prosecutor come out of the house, saying he was stabbed—I saw blood on his thigh.
FREDERICK DODSWORTH . I am a surgeon, and live at Turnham-green. On 18th July, I was called to attend the prosecutor—I found an incision in his thigh, four inches long, and about an inch deep—I have heard him describe that it was a dig, and drawn along—he being intoxicated it might be done in that way.
Cross-examined. Q. Would the cut sober him? A. Yes—this looked like a cut with the edge of a knife.
Witnesses for the Defence.
WILLIAM BLACKMAN . I keep the Old Pack Horse, at Turnham-green—I saw this row —I cannot say how the leg got cut—I never saw a knife used—I saw Allen strike him down in front of the bar, and knock his head through two squares of glass—it was between eight and nine o'clock when Allen came to my house—he was drunk—the prisoner and Thompson were there—Allen asked for half-a-quartern of gin, which I put in somen water—he said to me, "Blackman, you gave me two months once—you are a strong fellow in the house, if you say a word against me to-night I will rip your b----y guts out"—he rushed past me, and knocked down the prisoner in front of the bar—he knocked down Thompson and then the prosecutor's wife with a child in her arms—he then said, "I am wounded"—he pulled up his trowsers—there was a wound but no blood.
---- SAWYER. I am a livery-stable-keeper. I had been to Richmond and called at the Pack Horse that evening—I saw the whole of this—I can only tell the same as Mr. Blackman—after Allen made use of the expression of how he would serve Mr. Blackman, I stopped for Mr. Blackman's protection—I do not think Allen or the prisoner were sober—I did not see any knife—I saw the prisoner knocked through the window, and he wanted Allen to come out to fight—I saw Allen knock down two men, two women, and two children—he never said a word about being stabbed till he saw two more policemen come round the corner—he then threw himself down, and said he bad been stabbed—I called the attention of the policemen to it—there was a wound but no blood—I am certain I saw no knife.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18480821-1993]
1993. MARGARET ALLSTRUM , feloniously assaulting Edwin Allstrum, and wounding him on the head and throat, with intent to murder him.
ROBERT WILSON . I am surgeon at the workhouse of St. George's-in—the East. I saw the prisoner on 22nd July, she was labouring under an advanced stage of typhus fever, and was utterly unconscious of what she was doing.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18480821-1994]
1994. WILLIAM WARE , and JAMES GREEN , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Holliday, at St. Pancras, and stealing 1 key, value 1 s.; his property.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM HOLLIDAY . I live at 31, Cardington-street, Hampstead-road—I let the shop to Mr. Connington—I was awoke on 9th Aug., between twelve and one o'clock in the morning—I heard a rushing noise in my bed-room—the door was shut, I had locked it, and the key was outside—I had gone through a middle door to get into my bed-room—hearing the noise I said, "Who is there"—some one spoke—I got out of bed and called "Thieves!"—I saw two persons going out of the private door of my house—I saw two policemen at the same moment—they took them, and brought them back—I afterwards. missed the key with which they had locked me in my bed-room—that key was not found till after they had been to the station.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALFE. Q. Which side of Cardington-street is your house? A. It is the corner house, but it is rounded off—when the prisoners were brought back, the policeman first stopped at a house in Whittlebury-street, about six doors from mine—I had gone to bed about eleven o'clock the night before—I did up the doors—I saw the private door, it was latched, but was not bolted—I often go to bed with that door on the latch there are bolts on the door—I did not bolt it that night—it opens with a latch-key.
DAVID VEASEY CONNINGTON . I have the shop at No. 31, Cardington-street, of Mr. Holliday—the house is in St. Pancras parish—I was in the house on 10th August—I went to bed about half-past ten o'clock—I know Mr. Holliday shuts the door—I saw the prisoners brought back—both begged me not to lock them up, as they were customers—I said, "You are no customers of mine "—in going to the station, Ware broke away—I have no doubt they are the persons.
Cross-examined. Q. You keep a coffee-shop? A. Yes; I never saw the prisoners there.
GEORGE COOPER , ( policeman, S 276.) I was on duty about one o'clock ear Whittlebury-street with Downes—I saw the prisoners come out of No. 31,
Cardington-street—I heard the prosecutor call out from the window—he said there were thieves—I went into the Drummond Arms, and found both the prisoners there—I saw Green drop a key when I brought him out, which he took out of his right-hand pocket—it fits the bed-room door of No. 31.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it dark? A. Yes; but there was a lamp four or five yards off—I saw Green put his hand into his pocket and pull the key out—I went back and picked it up—I said at the office that I saw him drop a key—when I got to the Drummond Arms I saw both the prisoners, and perhaps thirty or forty persons—I did not inquire whether they had seen anybody—I went to another house, because I made a mistake in the house the prisoners came out of—I have not been reprimanded for not taking more notice of the house they came out of—there was a shop at the other house which I went to—I did not knock at that house—I cannot tell whether Downes did—I have not said before that I saw Green take something out—I said a key—if I had stooped to pick it up then, I should have been knocked down by some of the gang—there were a great number of cabmen, and thieves, and prostitutes—I did not lose sight of the prisoners before they ran into the Drummond Arms—I knew them before perfectly well.
WILLIAM DOWNES ( policeman, S 190.) I assisted to take the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. You saw two men come out of the house, and followed them to the Drummond Arms? A. Yes; I saw Ware I am certain—I knew him previously—the other I cannot swear to—when I went into the Drummond Arms, there were many persons sitting down and standing up—I said, "Mr. Oliver, where are those two men that ran in "—a railroad policeman said, "Here is one "—I looked in his face and said, "Yes, I know you are one "—I knew him before—I knocked at the other door—I was not certain which house they came out of—a man answered me out of the window—the prosecutor came up and said, had we got the two men—the prisoners said they were innocent.
ANN WALKER . I live in Southampton-street. On the 10th Ang. I was about two yards from the prosecutor's house—the two prisoners came out of the house close by me—they ran into the Drummond Arms, the policemen were going in at the side-door—I said, "Don't go in there, they have run in here."
Cross-examined. Q. Had you ever seen them before? A. No; they rushed past me, and nearly knocked me down—that made me take notice of them, and I ran after them—when I got to the public-house, they both rushed in.
REBECCA OLIVER . I am barmaid at the Drummond Arms—I saw some one pick up a key and give it to me—that was about a quarter to three o'clock—this is the key.
WILLIAM HOLLIDAY re-examined. This key produced by Cooper is my bed-room key—this last key I do not know.
WARE — GUILTY . Aged 24.
GREEN — GUILTY . Aged. 19
Transported for Ten Years.

1995. JANE SPEARING and ELIZABETH SPEARING , stealing 5lbs. weight of beef, 14oz. of suet, and 31bs. of potatoes, valae 3 s. 9 d.; the noods of Robert Cook, the master of Elizabeth; to which
ELIZABETH pleaded GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Four Months.
ROBERT COOK . I am a butcher at Sloane-square, Chelsea—the prisoner Elizabeth was in my service. On 13th Aug., about seven o'clock in the morning, she tapped at my bed-room door, and asked Mrs. Cook to let her have as much money as she could for her father—I gave her 6 s.—about a quarter to eight a message was sent to my wife who left the house, and I saw her go over to Mr. Forbes, and come back with a basket containing five pounds three ounces of beef, fourteen ounces of suet, three pounds of potatoes, one quart of scarlet beans, two pieces of soap, and a cloth enclosing the beef, with my initials on it—I can swear to the beef and suet—I cut it myself, and had the other portions in my shop—I found Elizabeth had left the house—I went to her mother's, and found both prisoners—Elizabeth said in Jane's presence that the basket was hers, but not the contents—the beef was safe at ten minutes past twelve the night before—the prisoner had no right to enter the shop.
JOHN FORBES . I am a perfumer of 14, Sloane-square. On 13th Ang., about seven o'clock, I was getting up, looked into the street, and saw the prisoner Jane with a basket—she waited in the street while a girl came out of Mr. Cook's, took the basket in, brought it out again, and gave it to Jane—I went across, and asked what she had in the basket—she said "nothing"—I said I should look—she would not let me—I said, "I am a policeman"—she opened the basket, and I saw the meat in it—I said I would take it, and give it to Mr. Cook when he got up—she asked me to go in and see her sister—I would not—I let her go—I gave Mrs. Cook the basket.
Jane's Defence. I thought the basket contained my sister's dirty clothes.
JANE SPEARING GUILTY . Aged 14.— Confined Two Months.

1997. WILLIAM JONES and JOHN TAYLOR , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of George Carrington, and stealing 1 desk, I ink-stand, 4 rings, and other articles, value 26 l. 1 l. 6 d.; the goods of Emma Ann Milton: and I desk, 5 seals, and other articles, value 16 l. 1 s.; the goods of Thomas Dean; to which
TAYLOR pleaded GUILTY , and received a good character. Aged 25.— Transported for Seven Years.
ANN QUARTERMAN . I am in the service of George Carrington, of Duke-street, Manchester-square—it is his dwelling-house. On 15th Aug., about ten o'clock at night, I was coming up the kitchen-stairs, and saw the prisoners standing at the front door inside—they could not open it, and when they saw me they rattled the chain very much—I ran up stairs, and called "Thieves!"—my master came.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. They had their backs to you? A. I saw Jones' face—he turned to me—I saw the side of the other's face—they had their hats on—I was very much frightened.
JOSEPH DOHERTY ( 'policeman, D 51). I heard a cry of "Police! "—I went fo the house, and found Jones crouched on one knee behind a clothes-horse, in a room at the back of the premises—Bailey was with me—I said, "Here
is one, and if you stir I will knock you down "—he jumped up, and struck me on the head with a life-preserver—I struck him with my staff—Bailey dropped the candle—we had a struggle, and fell—Sergeant Martin came, and we secured him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not you strike yourself on the head by warding off the blow with your arm? A. I put up my arm, but could not keen the blow off—I cut his head open with my staff.
JOHN BAILEY . I live at 21, Adam-street—I heard a cry of "Police!"—I went into the back kitchen and found the window-sash open—I saw a policeman in the stable—he called for assistance—I went—he was lying on Jones—I took Jones by the hand, took a life-preserver from him, put it into my pocket, and secured him—we were all in the dark.
JAMES MARTIN ( police-sergeant, D 2). I heard a cry, went in, and assisted in securing Jones—he was taken to the station, searched, and this property found on him ( produced).
EMMA ANN MILTON . I live in Mr. Carrington's house—this desk is mine—I saw the window shut down close half an hour before the alarm—the house is in Marylebone parish.
Cross-examined. Q. What time was it you saw the window shut? A. It was after nine o'clock—I am positive it was shut.
THOMAS DEAN . I live in the house—these seals are mine.
** JONES — GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for Ten Years.

1999. ANDREW BASCOVILLE and MARTHA RODERICK , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of George Samuel Russell, and others, and stealing 2 candlesticks, 1 teapot, 1 cream-jug, 1 pipe-bowl, and other articles, value 29 l. 3 s. 6 d.; his goods.
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE SAMUEL RUSSELL . I am a bootmaker, at 52, Fenchurch-street, in the parish of All-Hallows Staining. On 3rd Aug. my wife and I went to bed last, about a quarter to eleven or eleven o'clock—my house and property were quite safe—I was called up at a quarter to six, and found the sitting-room in a very disordered state—it adjoins a landing, where the window was broken open—the street-door was also open—I missed the property stated from that room—four locks were broken there—this bowl of a meersaaum pipe is mine—I had been smoking it in the sitting-room the evening before—Bascoville is my wife's nephew—it is twelve months or two years since I saw him—he knew my premises well—he used to visit there—I have no doubt that he knew that I kept my cash-box in that sitting-room.
Bascoville. Q. Why could not you swear to the pipe at the station. A. I scarcely had it in my hand.
GEORGE THOMAS RUSSELL , jun. I am the son of the last witness—I can swear positively to this pipe-bowl—I coloured it myself, which took me six or seven months—I gave it to my father a fortnight or three weeks before the robbery—I know it by these two flaws—I bought it cheaper on that account.
WILLIAM ROMAINE . ( policeman, L 38.) Between two and three o'clock is the day, on 12th Aug., I met Bascoville in the Waterloo-road—I called him by name, and said there was a charge against him in the city, and he must go with me to the Tower-street station—he denied the charge—I found
this bowl of a meershaum pipe on him ( produced)—I have seen Roderick with him several times—I have known them six or seven months.
HENRY WINCH . I am assistant to Mr. Barnes, pawnbroker, Circus, Blackfriars-road. On 3d Aug., between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, I took in this pipe-bowl of Roderick, in the name of Bennett—she redeemed it a few days afterwards—I also know Bascoville by sight—I never knew her by any name but Bascoville, but she said, "That is to go in the name of Bennett."
ANN PRICE . I live at 36, Borough-row—Roderick lodged in my house with a girl named Bennett—they came first about the end of July—I have seen Bascoville there—the last time was the morning he was taken—I have seen the prisoners together two or three times.
SARAH BENNETT . I am single, and live at Mrs. Price's—I know the prisoner Bascoville has called on Roderick three or four times at Mrs. Price's, and slept there twice with her. On 2nd Aug. Roderick was there with a friend—she told me a gentleman gave her this meershaum pipe—she had not seen Bascoville that night, I can be on my oath of it, nor did I.
WILLIAM EDMONS ( City-policeman, 528). I took Roderick in the Borough-road, and found a pair of Bascoville's shoes in the room—she said they were her brother's—Bascoville asked for them at the Mansion-house, and claimed them as his.
WILLIAM ROMAINE re-examined. This is the Lord Mayor's signature to this deposition—( read)—"Bascoville says, I have bought the pipe, at least I bought the ticket of it, and took it out of pawn at the pawnbroker's in the Surrey-circus, one day last week."
Bascoville's Defence. The pipe cannot be identified; the father used the pipe for a fortnight, and the colour alters by smoking; I have smoked it myself; Roderick told me she slept with a gentleman, who gave her the pipe, as he had no money; she gave me the ticket.
Roderick's Defence. A gentleman gave me the pipe, as he had not enough money; I pawned it nest day, and gave Bascoville the ticket.
NOT GUILTY .

2001. JAMES HUBON and WILLIAM COE , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Martha Ann Eliza Lightfoot, at St. Andrew, Holborn, and stealing 1 tablet, 1 thimble, and other articles, value 13 s.; her goods: and 1 hat, 1 pair of shoes, and other articles, value 5 l.; the goods of Augustus Bensback.
MR. PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
MARTHA ANN ELIZA LIGHTFOOT . I keep a lodging-house in Old Millman-street, in the parish of St. Andrew, Holborn—it is my dwelling-house. On 10th Aug., about ten o'clock in the morning, I missed a pencil-case and a lady's companion from a dressing-room, adjoining Dr. Bensback's room—these are them ( produced)—I found some lucifers there, and a pair of strange shoes, and gave them to the constable.
AUGUSTUS BENSBACK . I am a Doctor, and lodge at Miss Lightfoot's. On
9th Aug. I went to bed about a quarter past twelve o'clock—the house was safe as far as I know—I left some mould candles on the table—they were of the same height—next morning I missed two coats, a hat, pair of shoes umbrella, and two handkerchiefs—this coat and shoes are mine—( produced)—the shoes have been mended since.
EDWARD JOSEPH DAY . I lodged at Miss Lightfoot's. On 10th Aug. I came home two or three minutes before six o'clock in the morning—I let myself in with a latch-key, and found on the sideboard in the passage a bed-room candlestick, with a candle for me, and another candle lying flat on the sideboard—I left it there.
SARAH ELIZABETH OLIVER . I am in the service of Miss Lightfoot. I went to bed at about half-past eleven o'clock—the street door was closed, but left on the latch—I left a candlestick for Mr. Day with a candle in it, and some blue-tip lucifers by it—I left no mould candle by it—I got up about half-past six, went into Dr. Bensback's room, and missed a mould candle from one of the candlesticks—I found it in the passage—it was shorter than the other—all the doors and windows were secure.
FRANCIS MORRIS ( policeman). On 13th Aug., between seven and eight o'clock in the morning, I went to 15, Fullwood's-rents, and found Hubon in bed—I asked if he knew a woman named Coe—he said, "Yes, but he had not seen her a long time"—I asked if he had ever given or sold her any duplicates—he said, "No, I swear I dever did"—I said I was a policeman, and he must go with me for a robbery—he said to his mother, who was in the room, "You know I have never committed a night robbery; you know I have been in bed every night by ten o'clock"—I had not said anything about a night robbery, or any one else—I asked what he meant by alluding to a night robbery, as I had not mentioned it—he said I had, but he knew nothing about it—I found three duplicates, and a quantity of red-tipped matches in a dish-cover hanging against the wall—I received these similar matches from Miss Lightfoot—the prisoner said a man, who he did no know gave him the duplicates—by the time he was dressed, he had these shoes on—( produced)—I asked where he got them—he said he bought them in Petticoat-lane, but he did not know the man, and had had them mended since—I took him to the station, and told him he was charged with a burglary at 7, Millman-street, and the shoes he had on were the produce of the burglary—he said, "I don't see why I should suffer more than others"—I found 13 s. 8 1/2 d., and a latch-key on him, which will not open Miss Lightfoot's door—on 21st Aug., between five and six o'clock, I went to Martin's-buildings, Wandsworth, knocked at the door and saw Coe—I said, "Your name is Coe, is it not?"—he said, "No, sir, it is not"—I said, "I believe it is"—he denied it—I said, "You are the man I want, whether your name is Coe or not"—I said, "Hubon is in custody for a burglary, and I have found some duplicates on him; he says he received them from you"—he said, "I know nothing about it; let me go; indeed I don't"—he said all he knew about it was, he had met Hubon one morning, and he asked him to pledge a coat and pencil-case, which he did in Barbican, and gave Hubon the tickets and money—I said I had been looking for him a long while, and had ascertained that he had left London—he said he had not left London in consequence of the robbery, that it was his intention to appear at the Police-court, as he had heard he had been implicated in the affair by Hubon—I said to Hubon at the station, "You ought to have some shoes to put on"—I put him on those found at the house—they fitted him exactly.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Was it not that he heard his nam
had been implicated, and he had not left London in consequence? A. Yes—it was Coe's wife that gave me the information.
Hubon. Q. Did not you say to me, "You are charged with a robbery, committed the other night?" A. No—I saw the shoes before you were dressed, but waited till you put them on.
WILLIAM BOLTWOOD . I am in the service of Mr. Cotton, a pawnbroker, of Sboreditch. On 10th Aug., about twelve o'clock, Coe pawned a pencil-case in the name of John Eve—I asked whose it was—he said, "My own, I gave 8 s. 6 d. for it"—a female presented the ticket in the evening—this is it—( produced)—in about half an hour Hubon pledged this ladies' companion—I asked where he got it—he said be bought it at Margate for his sister, and gave half-a-guinea for it.
ROBERT UPSALL ,. I am a pawnbroker, of Barbican.—I took a coat in pledge on 10th Aug.—this is the ticket of it found on Hubon.
Hubon's Defence. If I had committed the robbery I should not have kept the boots eight days; they did not fit me.
(Coe received a good character.)
COE — GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
HUBON — GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18480821-2002]
2002. WILLIAM FLORENCE and ELIZA SMITH , for a robbery on Samuel Prout, and stealing from his person 1 watch, value 4 L., his goods; Florence having been twice before convicted of felony.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL PEOUT . I am a pianoforte-manufacturer, of Walcot-square. On 16th July, about two o'clock in the morning, I saw Smith near the Horse-guards—she asked me to treat her to a bottle of ginger-beer, and to go to see her apartments—we went to St. Ann-street, "Westminster—after being in the room three or four minutes, Florence knocked at the door, and inquired who sas there—she said, "I have a gentleman here"—he said, "Let me in"—she opened the door—he came in, and struck me a violent blow in the face—I kept the mark about nine days—they both got round me, and got my watch off the gold guard, and opened the window—I had seen it safe not a minute before I went in—they gave me a chance to escape, and I did so—I called, "Police!"—found one, two or three yards off, we went into the place, searched for the watch, but did not find it—it was worth 4 l. 10 s.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. Where had you been to? A. To the Hampstead-road on a visit—I had had some ale—I do not think I drank a pint and a half—I had no brandy—I did not quarrel with Smith—I paid her 1 s.—I did not strike her—I was sober.
WILLIAM NOWLAN ( policeman, B 56). I was on duty in St. Ann-street, about two o'clock—I heard "Police!"—I went in and saw Prout and the prisoners—Prout was bleeding—I saw Florence's arm in the act of throwing some-thing out of the window towards the bottom of the court—he had his head out of the window—I searched the room, and told another policeman to search the court—I afterwards took a lamp and searched a rubbish heap in the court—there were twenty or thirty thieves there who I knew—we found no watch—I returned to the room—Florence raised the window, saying it was rather warm, and bounced through it—I caught hold of his coat, it gave way, and he fell—I caught him in the next street—Prout was quite sober.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there any light in the room? A. Not before I entered—Florence said he heard a row in the room, and went to Smith's
assistance—Smith said she struck Prout because she would not comply without he gave her 1 s.
JOSEPH BUTCHER ( policeman). Nowlan left me to watch the door—I heard something thrown out of the window—it sounded like a watch—Lawrence asked if I had seen his shoe in the court—I swear that it was not a shoe that fell—I searched, but found no watch.
JOSEPH THOMPSON ( policeman). I produce a certificate—( read—John Smith, convicted Feb. 1845, and confined two months)—I was present—Florence is the man.
WILLIAM WEST . I produce a certificate—( read—John Smith, convicted Jan. 1847, and confined six months and six weeks, solitary)—I was present-Florence is the man.
FLORENCE — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.
SMITH — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.

2003. GEORGE HALL and WILLIAM STEWART , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Miles Fountain, and stealing 1 work-box, I purse, and other articles, value 3 l. 9 s. 6 d.; I halfsovereign and 5 shillings, the property of Harriett Miles; 1 work-bos, 1 thimble, and other articles, value 1 s. 6 d., 3 half-crowns and 6 s., the property of Ann Fletcher; I work-box, 2 thimbles, and other articles, value 9 s., and 1 half-crown, the property of Eleanor Hasler.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES HAWKER ( police-sergeant, D 9). On 29th July, about half-past four in the morning, I saw the prisoners coming along Marylebone-street towards Oxford-street—they each had a bundle—they looked at me—I went to the station, got assistance, and followed them into Woodstock-street—I asked Hall what he had got—he said "These," holding up a handkerchief, and that he had brought them from Notting-hill, that they belonged to him, and he was moving them himself to prevent their being scratched—we took them both to the station—I said to Hall, "You say these boxes belong to you?"—he said, "Yes"—I said, "Who is this?" pointing to Stewart—he said, "My brotber-in-law"—I said, "If these boxes belong to you, you have got the keys of them"—he said, "Yes, these two keys open three of the boxes," and gave them me from his pocket—I said, "Whose box is this?"—he said, "My sister's"—I said, "What is in it?"—he said, "Tapes, cottons, and other things"—the key opened it—it contained those sort of things—I said, "How is it you had it at your place if it belonged to your sister?"—he said, "She was in service at Porchester-terrace"—I found several letters in it addressed "Porchester-terrace"—I said. "Who does this box belong to?"—he said, "That is my wife's"—I said, "What is in it?—he said, "The same sort of things, I can't tell you exactly; I don't bother myself about my wife's things"—I opened it, it contained similar things to the other, and a letter addressed "Miss Fletcher, Porchester-terrace"—I asked who Miss Fletcher was—he said a friend of his wife's—I found these purses in another box, one with money in it—I said, "Here is a purse, what money is in it?"—he said, "You will find three half-crowns and some silver"—I found three half-crowns and six shillings—another constable took two boxes out of Hall's pocket—I said, "Who does this one belong to?"—he said, "To his sister"—I said to Stewart, "Does this belong to your sister?"—Stewart said, "Yes"—I opened it, it was quite full of tea—Hall said, he filed it full before he left—I said, "Who does this box belong to?"—Hall said, "That is my own, the key won't open that"—I said, "Where is the key?"—he said, "It is on my other keys, which I have left
behind"—I sent to Notting-hill to make inquiries—I then charged the prisoners on suspicion, and went to Porchester-terrace about half-past eight or a quarter to nine, and found a pair of steps inside the garden, which would enable a person to get down from the garden-wall—there was a quantity of grass put on the bottles on the wall—I traced footsteps from the wall to a window of the house—the window was broken, which enabled any one to undo the fastening and throw up the sash—this iron bar was given me by the gardener—the bar could be bent with it and the shutters opened—the bar is bent—the house is in Paddington parish.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Did you caution him that he need not answer your questions? A. No; because he said the property was his—I have been a sergeant nearly ten years, and have often asked a great many more questions—I found two distinct footmarks, and several others—I should say they were footmarks of more than one person—I did not compare the prisoner's boots with them.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Can you swear there were foot-marks of more than one person? A. No—they did not run when I met them—they were half a mile out of their way, if going from Notting-hill to Blackfriars-road.
JOSEPH GILBERT ( policeman, D 177). I was called by Sergeant Hawker, and found the prisoners—I asked what they had got about them—Stewart said he had two tea-caddies and some work-boxes—I searched him, and found these two boxes in his coat pocket ( produced), and on Hall I found a purse with a half-crown, three penny pieces, and a counterfeit farthing—Hall said he lived at Uxbridge-street, Notting-hill—I went to Notting-hill about six o'clock, and asked whether Hall lived there—that alarmed the family—they were asleep.
HARRIET MILES . I am in the service of John Miles Fountain, of Porchester-terrace, Bayswater. About half-past six in the morning, a policeman rung the bell—I came down, and found the kitchen window open, and missed some boxes—three of these boxes belong to me and another to my fellow-servant Fletcher—I had gone to bed at half-past ten, and left the house fastened up. and the kitchen-window secured—I found the bar removed and bent, and this piece of iron laid on the lawn—it belonged to the brewhouse—it could have been put through the place in the window to bend the bar.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Nothing was missed but from the kitchen? A. No.
(Edward Grummer, eating-house keeper; and Sarah Bailey, both of Banbury; William Wittle, tailor, of Devereaux-street; and Mary Parish, gave Hall a good character. Stewart also received a good character.)
HALL — GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
STEWART — GUILTY .— Recommended to mercy.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18480821-2004]
2004. WILLIAM KNIGHT and GEORGE WARD , were indicted for a robbery on Thomas Ebenezer Evans, and stealing from his person 1 watch, value 3 l.; his goods.
THOMAS EBENEZER EVANS . I am a shoemaker, of Augusta-street, Regent's-park. On 3rd July, about nine in the evening, I went into the George, public-house, at Hampstead, with a friend—the prisoner came in and left at the same time as us—Knight asked me the time—I pulled out my watch and said it was half-past nine—I had a watch-guard—just before we got to the "Load of Hay" Knight said, "Will you have some gin?"—we went in there—the prisoner came out with us—Knight asked me the time again—I took out my watch and said, "Twenty minutes to ten"—we came to a
dead wall—Knight snatched at my watch—I called out "He has got my watch"—he did not get it—he closed on me—Ward called out, "Throw the b---- Bill; if you don't I will"—Knight put bis foot up, I went down he kicked me in the knee, and pot my watch—I got up, and he kicked me in the ribs—I had hold of him round the body—my friend kept "Ward away—a policeman came—I found a part of my chain hanging round my neck—this is the watch ( produced).
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Who was with you? A. Bannister, a painter—we had been together since half-past one at Hampstead—we were in two or three public-houses—I was slightly intoxicated, but knew what I was about—Knight was slightly intoxicated—they spoke to me first, about the time—I do not recollect Knigh saying at the "Load of Hay" that I took a half-crown out of his pocket—I swear I took nothing out of his pocket—he did not before he struggled with me ask me for the money I had taken from him.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. How many public-houses did you go to on the Heath? A. To the Spaniards—I—was not arm in arm with Knight—I was on his left side—I never said I was on the right—I heard nothing about a half-crown—I saw Ward put down his umbrella and walking-stick on the pavement, and come back to Knight—next day my friend pointed Ward out at the police-office, and he was taken.
THOMAS WILLIAM BANNISTER . I am a ainter, of Bath-place. On 23rd July, I went with Evans to Hampstead—we saw the prisoners in the "George"—as we came out Knight asked him the time—he told him—we went on to the Load of Hay, had some gin, came out, and, half-way down the hill, Evans cried out, " Tom, he has got my watch!"—Evans was on the outside of Knight, we were all close together—he had not got the watch—he made a snatch at it, and they closed—Ward cried out, "Throw the b----, Bill; if you don't I will"—he laid down his umbrella and stick and was going up to help Knight, I stopped him, called "Police!" and gave him in charge—I saw the watch in Knight's hand as they fell the second time—Evans gave him in charge—he said, "I hope you will not give me in custody; it will get me into a deal of trouble"—I did not hear him say anything about a half-crown—they got away from the policeman—Knight fell over a stone, and was taken—I did not see Ward till next day.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Was not Knight pretty drank? A. He knew what he was about—I was perfectly sober—Knight said nothing about the half-crown to me—he said so at the office—this is my signature to my deposition—it was read over to me before I signed it—( read —" Knight said Evans had robbed him of half-a-crown, and Ward said so too")—that is true—Ward said he had been robbed of half-a-crown, and it was put back again when the policeman came up, but I forgot it—I heard both say it—we only had half a pint of gin between four of us at the Load of Hay—the stick belonged to Knight, but Ward had it.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did not the prisoners stand as much drink as you did at the George? A. No—we walked four abreast, but not arm in arm—Knight was between Evans and Ward, who was on Knight's left—when Knight was on the ground, he or Ward said, "I will not be robbed of half-a-crown, you have robbed me and put it back again into my pocket"—I said to Evans, "Feel in your pocket"—I don't know that any half-crown was found—I did not see Knight with half-a-crown—I think Ward had had the most dink—they were all three in liquor—I was as sober as I am now.
ALBERT AUSTIN ( policeman, S 369). I heard a noise on Haverstock-bill, and found Knight and one or two others near the Load of Hay—I saw them
fall, went up and got Knight off—Ward and Bannister were in the road—Ward said he had lost half a crown from his pocket, and it had been put back again, and that Knight was quite innocent—Evans gave Knight in charge—he said he was innocent—about 100 yards from the turnpike he ran away, and Ward also—Knight fell, and I overtook him, took him to the station, but found nothing on him—I went back to Haverstock-hill, and found this watch ( produced) about a yard from the footpath, where they had stood—the ring was broken off, and lying by the side of it—Bannister was sober, Evans was the worse for liquor—the prisoners were both intoxicated.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Knight denied having the watch, and said all he wished for was justice between the two? A. Yes.
JOSEPH PRICE ( policeman, D 240). On 24th July, while Knight was under examination, I took Ward at the back of the Court—I followed him to the cells, and beard Knight say, "Tell my friends where I am"—I said to Ward, "Were you in company with the soldier, yesterday?"—he said yes, he was at the George public-house, and left him there, but he knew nothing of the robbery—I said, "I believe the officer wants to see you"—he said, "No, he does not"—I said, "I believe he does"—we had some conversation—he gave me his address and the key of his room—I went there and found this stick and the coat which he had had on, all over mad and dirt—it was a very wet night.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18480821-2009]
2009. JONATHAN GAYDON , WILLIAM PERKISS , and WILLIAM CANSON , stealing 1 cow, value 15 l.; the property of James Gaydon.
JAMES GAYDON . I am a milk-man, of Walthamstow. I had a cow, called "Beauty," safe in my field on Thursday evening, 10th Aug.—I would not have taken under 15 l. for it—I had not sold it, nor suffered my son to sell it—I missed it a few minutes before six o'clock next morning—I afterwards saw it at Temple Mills, nearly two miles from my premises—Mr. Love's man had it.
ROBERT KEDGE . I am a carpenter. On the evening of 10th Aug., I saw Canson—he asked me if the cow-boy was on the premises—I saw him again about a quarter past eight o'clock, with the other two prisoners, about 100 yards from Gaydon's place—Jonathan Gaydon said if I saw the old gentleman I was not to tell him I had seen him—the other two prisoners were near enough to hear that.
GEORGE HUNTER ( policeman, N 357). On Thursday evening, 10th Aug., I saw Perkins and Canson about twenty minutes before nine o'clock, about a mile from the prosecutor's, driving the cow before them—I asked where they were going to take it—they said, "To Temple Mills, to go to Smithfield"—I asked where they got it—Perkiss said, "Bought it, and paid for it, of young Gaydon"—I said, "Which?"—Perkiss replied, "Of the father and the son both, on the Monday previous," but Mr. Gaydon had given them leave to let it remain there till Thursday—Perkiss said he gave 8 l. for it, and he had been at the Nag's Head with Gaydon and his son, and they had a pot of ale together—I saw Mr. Gaydon the next morning, and from what he said I took Perkiss into custody—the cow was found in possession of one of Mr. Love's men.
Cross-examined. Q. Is Mr. Love here? A. No—I know the prisoners by sight—one lives at Stratford, and the other at Hackney—they are licensed drovers, and drive through there—the gate of the field where this cow was kept is kept locked—it opens into the road.
JAMES M'CULLOCH ( policeman N 36). On Friday morning, 11th Aug., I went with the prosecutor to try to find the cow—I saw Mr. Love's man with it—I left it at Temple Mills, in charge of the police—it was afterwards delivered to Mr. Gaydon—I took Perkiss into custody in Smithfield—he said he bought it of young Gaydon, on the Monday previous, for 3 l., and gave a deposit of 2 s., that he came on the Thursday night and paid the 3 l. to him, and he let him in at the small gate with a latch-key, and drove the cow out at the large gate; and allowed him to keep the 2 s. extra as he bad got a good bargain.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Mr. Love? A. Yes; he was before the Magistrate—he advanced 4 l. to purchase the cow—he told me he bought it—he lives nearly three miles from the prosecutor's.
JAMES FINCH ( policeman, N 355). I took Gaydon on the evening of 11th Aug.—I cautioned him in the usual way—I told him I took him for being concerned in the robbery of his father's cow—he cried very much, and said he should not have done it if he had not been persuaded by some person to buy a gun, and had not the money to do it.
WILLIAM SPINKS ( policeman, N 276). I took Canson—I told him he was chargd with stealing a cow—he said he did not steal it, and had no hand in purchasing it; but Purkiss saw him in Smithfield, and asked him if he would go to Walthamstow, where there was a job; and when the cow was sold to Mr. Lowe, he received 1 l. 10 s. as his share of the profit.
JAMES GAYDON re-examined. I never saw these men on the subject of this cow—I was not with them at the Nag's Head, and received 8 l., and had a pot of ale with them and my son.
Cross-examined. Q. You have three or four sons? A. I have three.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Serjcant.

2014. WILLIAM SIMPSON , stealing 30 feet of iron pipe, value 16 s.; the goods of the Eastern Counties Railway Company; having been before convicted.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM COX NOURSE . I am a gas-fitter, of Stratford-grove, Essex. This pipe was safe at the Stratford station three weeks before the 4th Aug.—it is the property of the Eastern Counties Railway Company—the prisoner worked for the contractors, not for the company.
WILLIAM JONES . I am foreman to Mr. Foy, contractor for gas to the Eastern Counties Railway Company. The prisoner was formerly in his employ—I saw him crossing the line from the Stratford workshops towards Stratford, on 4th Aug., with these three pieces of pipe—I asked where he got them, he said he was going to the gas-works, to Drury—I said, "Drury does not want it"—he said, "I understand he has a job; I brought it up from Blackwall; it is not your pipe"—I said, "It is; I wish to know who it does belong to"—he said, "Where is Mr. Foy?"—I said, "On the platform"—he went to him, and Mr. Foy gave him in charge—we missed the pipe—I produce a piece corresponding with it.
JAMES GALE . I am a gate-keeper at Stratford. On 4th Aug. I took the prisoner—he said he purchased the pipes of Russell and Co., Upper Ground-street, Blackfriars—I took him there, and found they were not bought there—it is galvanized iron tubeing.
ERASMUS JAMES NICHOL LAWRENCE . I transact the business of Messrs. Russell, and have the selling of all the goods. I did not sell the prisoner these pieces of pipe—it would not be sold in this state—it is galvanized, but the prisoner said it was painted.
Prisoner. Q. Do not you recollect me coming there? A. Several times, I think, but I do not recollect your purchasing anything.
Prisoner's Defence. The foreman of the ticket-takers saw me with it in Stratford, and if I had stolen it I should not have returned with it to the railway.
JOSEPH HENRY TAYLOR ( policeman, D 115). I produce a certificate—I was at the trial—the prisoner is the man mentioned in it—( read—Convicted Feb., 1846, having been before convicted: confined six months).
GUILTY . Aged 50. Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18480821-2015]
2015. ROBERT ELLIOTT , for feloniously wounding John Roscoe on the head, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN ROSCOE . I am bailiff at the County Court, Greenwich. On Wednesday, 26th July, I put a man into possession of the prisoner's house, at Plampstead—in consequence of a communication, I went to the house, between five and six o'clock in the afternoon—when I entered the house I saw the prisoner standing at the door—I said I was a bailiff of the County Court, and had a warrant to seize his goods—I produced the warrant to him—he said, "Get out of my house, you d----vagabond; if you don't I will kill you"—he had something concealed behind him, and he made a job at my eye—it turned out to be a red-hot poker—it burnt my ear, and all my hair off there—he then struck me a violent blow with it at the back of the head, which caused a wound, and a great quantity of blood to flow—Holmes, who was with me, tried to take the poker from him, and he struck Holmes over the hat at the back of the head, which made a large dent in his hat—we at length succeeded in getting the poker from him—I went to a surgeon.
Cross-examined by MR. COOK EVANS. Q. Where did you first see the prisoner? A. At the back-door—he occupies the back part of the house, which stands in a yard—I was in the wash-house when I first saw him—I did not offer him the warrant to read—I did not read it to him—he did not give me the opportunity—he swore he would kill me if I dared to come near him—he appeared in a violent rage—I do not know "whether he was drunk—he was on the steps when he struck me—I was rather below him—there was no fire in the washhouse—I do not know where he had come from—I had not been talking to him a moment before this occurred—I was laid up for a week after—I went and got a cart to remove his things, and went about my business next day—I thought very little of it—Holmes was standing close to me when he was struck—he had gone with me.
ALFRED SEPTIMUS HOLMES ( policeman, R 93). I accompanied Roscoe, and saw him produce his warrant—the prisoner asked him what he wanted, and immediately struck him twice with the poker, once on the side of the face, and once on the back of the head—the last blow caused a great discharge of blood—I interfered, to take the poker' from him, and he struck me with it, which bruised my hat, and made a lump on my head.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you present when Roscoe went in ? A. Yes, I was standing against the wash-house door—he produced the warrant—he had not time to hand it to the prisoner, because he struck him directly—the prisoner did not ask for it, he saw it.
DAVID HOLMES . I am a surgeon at Deptford-bridge—Roscoe came to me next morning—I found a scar on the left ear. and at the back of the head a wound about half an inch long—I did not probe it, as he had been to a surgeon before.
Cross-examined. Q. The skin was broken? A. Just broken—it was at
a dangerous part—a sudden blow there might dislocate the head—I washed it and dressed it—I should say it was such an injury as might have been inflicted with a poker.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY of an Assault. — Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18480821-2016]
2016. MARY ANN DALE , stealing 1 half-crown, 6 shillings, and 2 sixpences, of Henry Greenwood, from his person.
HENRY GREENWOOD . I am thirteen years old, and live with my father and mother at Black-horse-place, Lower-road, Deptford—last Saturday week, I took half a pint of ale to Mr. Temple's, and he gave me half a sovereign to get changed—I got half-a-crown, 6 shillings, 2 sixpences, 5 1/2 d. in copper, and a halfpenny-worth of plums—the prisoner met me—I go of errands for her—she asked me if her husband was in our place—I said I did not think he was—she said, "Come along with me"—I went with her down Effingham-place—she knocked me down with her hand, knelt on me, put her hand over my mouth, forced my hand open, and took the money out, and said, "Don't you think I have robbed you," and then put the halfpence into my hand and run away—another boy was with me—it was about eight o'clock at night—I watched her home and told a policeman—he took her next day.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did not you accuse some one of taking 1 s., which you had for some beer, and you said you had not got it, and your mother found it on you? A. No—I do not know Scam, I have seen that man before ( looking at one)—he never gave me 1 s., which I denied his giving to me, which was found on me—this was quite an open place—I did not tell Eliza Tripps that it was a tall big woman in a white shawl and a black bonnet—I do not know Tripps—I said she was a little woman with a black velvet bonnet and a light shawl—my father said if I did not get the woman I should catch it—I had never played with Holmes before—I had seen him—I do not know that he has been convicted—there is a large gas-lamp at the corner of Effinghain-place, the prisoner lives a little way from there.
JOSEPH HENRY HOLMES . I am twelve years old, and live in Cross-street, Deptford—I was by the Prince Regent door about eight o'clock one day last week, and saw a woman in a light shawl and black bonnet knock Greenwood down—he had 5 1/2 d. in his hand.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you pick him up? A. I caught hold of his hand, and he jumped up—I have been in prison at Brixton—we had not spent the money between us.
WILLIAM THOMAS ARTHUR ( policeman, R 202). On Saturday night about nine o'clock Greenwood complained to me—I went with him to different houses, but could not find the party—on Sunday evening about six he came and took me to the prisoner's house—she was standing at the door—I asked her if a female had been in there ten minutes or quarter of an hour ago, she said, "No"—I said, "Have you lodgers?" she said, "No"—she was very much confused—Greenwood said, "That is the woman you want"—I said, "Are you sure of that?"—he said, "Quite positive"—I asked the prisoner if she knew him—she said, "I believe he lives at the beer-shop over the way "—he said, "Yes, you know I do, you are the party that robbed me "—I asked her to produce the bonnet and shawl she had on yesterday—she put on a light shawl and black bonnet.
Cross-examined. Q. You had no notion she was the person? A. Decidedly not—I have known her for years—she is a very respectable woman—
she said he was a very wicked boy for saying she was the woman—her house is forty or fifty yards from the spot—the beer-shop is two hundred yards further on—it is a public place.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

2017. BRIDGET LLOYD , stealing 1 sheet, 1 petticoat, and 2 yards of drugget, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Robert Baldery.
ROBERT BALDERY . I am a baker, at Woolwich. On 11th July, about twelve o'clock at night, I lost some sheeting and petticoats—I received them from my daughter, next morning—these are them ( produced).
HARRIET BALDERY . I am sixteen years old. I met the prisoner in, Wellington-street—she was not a friend of mine—she said she took some clothes, and if I would go with her to her house she would show them me—I went, and found the petticoat and drugget in her yard—they are my father's.
Prisoner's Defence. I found the things in the yard, and took them in; next morning I forgot to say anything about them.
ROBERT BALDERY re-examined. It is a mixed yard, not ours only.
NOT GUILTY .

2020. JAMES BARNES , stealing 3 pewter-pots, value 3 s.; the goods of John Munn.
JOHN MUNN . I keep the Hare and Billet, at Blackheath. These pots are mine ( produced)—I lost them—the prisoner lived two or three hundred yards from me.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Does he-deal with you? A. Yes—he is a blacksmith—he has fetched beer for his supper, and my potman has fetched the pots in the morning—I put two of these pots by my stable-door, and one across the road.
JOHN TAYLOR ( policeman). On 31st July, between five and six o'clock in the morning, I secreted myself, and saw the prisoner come from his forge, and look about him—he picked up a pot from the road, near the tip-room window, put it under his apron, and returned towards his house—three pots were standing in the road—he took two, put them under his apron, went to his forge, and locked the door—my brother constable knocked—he did not open
it—the half-door was not fastened—we went in, and found one pint and two quart pots on a bench.
Cross-examinrd. Q. Is not he quite deaf? A. Yes—he has lived there respectably more than nine years—you could reach the pots from the door.
NOT GUILTY .

2023. CATHERINE HERRION , stealing 1 half-crown, 11 d., 2 half-pence, and 1 farthing; the goods of James Figg; having been before convicted.
HARRIET FIGG . I am the daughter of James Figg. On 30th June, about nine o'clock, the prisoner and another young woman came and called for two glasses of porter—my father brought it—I saw the prisoner put her hand into the till, take some halfpence, and put them into her pocket—I missed half-a-crown from the till—I saw her take some more halfpence off the counter—I told my father.

[trialAccount t18480821-2024]
2024. WILLIAM JOHNSON and THOMAS WILLIAMS , stealing 1 purse, value 1 s.; 4 shillings, 2 sixpences, and 3 groats, the property of John Rees; from the person of Eliza D'Oyley Rees.
ELIZA D'OYLEY REES . I am the wife of John Rees. On 14th July, I was at Sir Thomas Wilson's fete, at Charlton—an officer spoke to me—I missed my purse from my right-hand pocket—there were shillings, sixpences, and fourpenny pieces in it—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALFE. Q. How do you know it? A. I have had it two years—I looked at it before I left my house.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Had you seen the prisoners? A. Yes, in front of the garden—not three minutes before White spoke to me.
JOHN WHITE ( policeman, R 180). I was on duty at Sir Thomas Wilson's on the afternoon of 14th July, there was a fete there—I saw the prisoners together, watched them about a quarter of an hour, and saw them go both together, and stand beside Mrs. Rees—Williams lifted her pocket, or her dress—Johnson covered him—they then left—I went and spoke to her, and then took Johnson—I said, "Hand me over that purse"—he said he had no purse—he then took it from his pocket, and threw it on the ground—I called to my brother officer to take it up.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALFE. Q. Did you see it when he took it out? A. Yes, and tried to get it—there was not much of it visible—I saw it was something red—when it was on the ground I could see that it was a purse—Gladwin picked it up—I was tustling with the prisoner—I saw a form with ladies and gentlemen on it—Mrs. Rees stood with her back to it—there were persons in front of her—there were persons on the form looking over her head, and of course over Williams's head.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Were you behind Williams? A. Yes—Johnson hid him—I took Johnson from twenty-five to thirty yards from the spot.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( policeman, R 122). I was with the other officer at Sir Thomas Wilson's—I saw the prisoners together—Williams went to Mrs. Rees, and apparently put his hand into her pocket—Johnson covered him—Williams took something from the pocket, and gave it to Johnson, who put it into his pocket, and they both walked away—I spoke to Mrs. Rees—I then saw Johnson in White's custody—I took Williams—White said, "Look out, he has dropped the purse on the ground"—I found this purse on the ground—it has shillings, sixpences, and fourpenny pieces in it.
JOHN CARPENTER ( policeman, R 4). I was on duty, and watched the prisoners—Williams attempted to pick a lady's pocket—he then went behind Mrs. Rees—I stood behind the form, and separated two ladies' dresses with my hand, looked through, and saw Williams take from Mrs. Rees's pocket a red purse—he had a handkerchief in his hand wiping his mouth with it—he wrapped it in the handkerchief, and passed it to Johnson—I took this handkerchief from Williams—I heard Johnson say he had no purse.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALFE. Q. Did you state this before the Magistrate? A. Yes, on cross-examination—I was in plain clothes—I belong to the Detective Force of the R division—there were five or six thousand persons there.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Were you behind the others? A. I was behind the form which was between me and Mr. Rees; I have been a policeman nearly twelve years—I believe that I did right to state that I saw Williams try one pocket before—I believe it is my duty to state all I saw the prisoners do on that occasion—I should not have allowed them to commit more than one robbery if I could have helped it, but if I could not, I should have stated what I had seen—I am aware that to attempt is an offence, and a summary conviction would have been the consequence—the form was five or six feet long—I was about the middle of it, or if anything, rather nearer to the end where the prisoners were—it was perhaps a yard and a half from Mrs. Rees—I was looking to her left side.
JOHNSON — GUILTY . Aged 34.— Transported for Seven Years.
WILLIAMS — GUILTY . Aged 14.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18480821-2025]
2025. GEORGE HUGHES , stealing 1 coat, value 3 s., the goods of William Charles Dewick.
WILLIAM CHARLES DEWICK . I am a coachman of Blackhead). I was in the service of the proprietor of the Green Man. On 16th Aug. last year, I put my coat in a carriage in the coach-house about half-past one or two o'clock in the clay—I missed it about seven at night—I found it next morning at Mr. Sharp's, a pawnd-oker, at Deptford—I got it out,. and. I have it on now—it is mine—I know the prisoner by sight—I cannot swear that I had seen him that day.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was there anything particular going on at Blackheath that day? A. Yes, a foot-race—there were many people about, and many people came in the yard no doubt—I fetched the tailor to swear to my coat before I took it out, but I had no doubt of its being mine—he said he made it for me—there was an old silk handkerchief in the pocket, and the coat was tied up in that handkerchief, which I knew, and had had a long time.
JOHN ACKLAND . I am a coachman. I was in the employ of the proprietor of the Green Man—I recollect the foot-race Last Aug.—the prisoner came there about five o'clock—I heard Dewick speak about this business that day.
Cross-examined. Q. What makes you recollect that the prisoner came that day? A. He was a fellow-servant of mine, and came to see me—I did not see him go away—I left him to go to see the race—I know he came down on the day of the race to fetch some little things he had left at the Green Man with me—I have seen him once since.
JAMES SHARP . I am an assistant to Joseph Sharp, a pawnbroker at Deptford. On 16th Aug. last year, a coachman's blue coat, of this description, was pawned with me, I do not recollect by whom—the person had this ticket given him—I let Dewick have it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you give it up next day? A. My employer did; I was present—I am quite sure it was the coat that was pawned the day before—I did not see the duplicate again till it was brought on 30th June this year—I had not the coat to produce then.
RICHARD MINOR . I am in the service of Mr. Riches, of Clapham-common. I saw the prisoner at a public-house three or four months ago—he asked if I wanted a coat—I said I did not know that I did, what sort of coat was it—he said a blue frock coat, with velvet collar, and pockets at the sides—I said, "How much do you want for it?"—he said, "I don't want to sell it, it is in pawn at Deptford, but I will give you the duplicate, provided you will et it out"—I said, "How do you know it will fit me"—he said, "Because it fits me, and you are about the same stamp" —I said, "Then you knew the man it belonged to?"—he said, "Yes, he was a groom, who left service, and took a cab, and is now gone to Australia "—he gave me this duplicate—I kept it till 30th June—I sent it to the pawnbroker by my nephew.
JAMES MINOR . I took the duplicate to the pawnbroker.
JOHN CARPENTER ( policeman, R 84). I took the prisoner—I asked him if he pawned a coat at Mr. Sharp's—he said he had not—I asked him if he had had the ticket of a coat pawned at Mr. Sharp's—he said, "No"—I said, "Did you not gave a ticket of a coat to a man named Minor, living at Clapham?"—he said, "Yes, I did "—he said he had the duplicate of a man he did not know at a public-house in the Kent-road for a pot of beer.
NOT GUILTY .

2025. RICHARD ILES , stealing 1 spoke-shave, value 2 s.; the goods of Robert Fitch; 1 hammer, 1 screw-driver, and 1 pair of compasses, value 4 s. 6 d.; the goods of Samuel Browning.
ROBERT FITCH . I am a carpenter, and was working at Berners-street, Greenwich. The prisoner was working there, in the employ of the contractor—on 14th Aug. I lost this spoke-shave ( produced)—I am sure it is mine.
SAMUEL BROWNING . I was working at the building—I lost a screw-driver and pair of compasses—these are them.
Prisoner. Q. Were not you, and I, and the builder's son, drinking all day from eleven o'clock till we left off? A. Part of the time we were—I was tipsy, and fell between the joists.
JOHN CARPENTER ( policeman, 84 R). I received information, went to a pawnbrokers, and saw these tools handed to the prisoner—he paid money for them—he took part of them—I took the rest—I said, "These are the things I am in search of, and I am in search of you also for stealing them"—he said, "I am very sorry, I pawned them here on Monday; if you will allow me I will take them back."
Prisoner's Defence. I went to work; they called me down to pay my footing; I had but 1 s. 6 d.; I spent that, and a young man said, "Can't we raise some money by taking some tools?"—Browning authorised me to pawn them—I said I would get them again.
SAMUEL BROWNING re-examined. I paid for part that I drank, but these things were pawned afterwards—I left a boy to take my tools in—the boy was moving the boards, and I slipped down—the prisoner denied having had the tools the next morning.
The Prisoner called
WILLIAM ELLIS . I called at the house about half-past two o'clock—the prisoner, and the plasterers and carpenters were all drinking together—Browning was so much intoxicated he could not stand.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18480821-2026]
2026. ARTHUR LEARY and RICHARD COOPER , unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DAWSON conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZA PERRIAN . My husband keeps the Joiner's Arms, beer-shop, at Lewisham. On 20th July, about twelve o'clock in the day, the prisoner Cooper came and asked for half a pint of porter—it came to a penny—he gave me a bad half-crown—I looked at it, threw it down, and told him it was bad—he snatched it up instantly, and gave me a good one—he went out and I sent a boy to follow him—I saw Cooper join Leary and another—they went to Lee.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How far were the other persons from you? A. About 100 yards—I had never seen Cooper before—he was at our place about two minutes.
ELIZABETH BOOKER . My husband keeps the Highlander, at Lee, about a mile from the Joiner's Arms. On 26th July, Leary came there about half-past twelve o'clock, and asked for a pint of beer which came to twopence—he gave me a bad half-crown—I gave him the change—he left—I thought it was not good, and laid it on a shelf—there was no other money there—my brother was standing at the door—I called his attention to the half-crown, about ten minutes after Leary was gone—he took it and went after Leary.
Cross-examined. Q. Where do you generally put money? A. In the
till—I suspected at first it was bad—I am sure I got it from Leary—no customer came in from the time I placed it on the shelf, till I asked my brother about it—I did not throw it in a place where there was other money—there is a bowl kept in the till, which is a drawer in the bottom shelf of the cupboard—when I receive money I go to the cupboard—I laid the half-crown on the third shelf, distinct from the till.
WILLIAM BOOKER . On 26th July my sister gave me this half-crown—I saw it was bad—I went in pursuit of Leary—I saw him and Cooper, about ten rods off on the right, on the road to Lee—I followed and cave them in charge at the Tigier's Head—on going along the road they fell in conversation with another man—I gave the half-crown my sister gave me, to the sergeant.
JAMES JENKINS ( police-sergeant, R 10). Booker came to me—I went to the Tiger's Head, and saw the prisoners in conversation—the other man had left hem—Leary was about to enter the Tiger's Head—I took him and Cooper to the station—I found this half-crown in Leary's hand—I was about to search Cooper—he took his right hand from his pocket, and in it was this half-crown—I bent the two half-crowns between my teeth, and found they were bad—I found a penny on each of the prisoners—this is the half-crown I received from Booker.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These three half-crowns are all counterfeit, and have all been cast in the same mould.
LEARY — GUILTY . Aged 37.
COOPER — GUILTY . Aged 44.
Confined One Year.
