2041. MARY ANN STAPLES , breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Richard Eldridge, and stealing 2 half-crowns, 3 s., and 5 sixpences; his moneys.
RICHARD ELDRIDGE . I live in Alfred-place, Battersea. On 22nd Aug., at twenty-five minutes before five in the morning, I left my room to go to work, leaving in the table-drawer two bags containing the money stated—I double locked the door—I returned about eight in the evening, and my money was gone, also some bread and butter and sugar—I have known the prisoner from her infancy—she was born in the same yard, but was never in my room—one of my half-crowns had a mark on it by which I know it—this is it ( produced)—I told the policeman the mark that was on it before I examined it ( pointing out the mark)—the staple of the door had been forced away, as if by a chisel.
MARY ANN BIRD . The prisoner lodged with me on Friday evening last—she only slept there one night—it was out of charity—this chisel ( produced) is my husband's—it was kept in a drawer—the prisoner might have taken it without my knowing it while I was out at work.
THOMAS DAVIS ( policeman). I examined the door—there were marks of violence which appear to have been made with this chisel, which was brought to me from a drawer by Mrs. Bird.
MRS. BIRD re-examined. My husband was in the country on this occasion—another young woman lodged in the house, but she was at Wandsworth then—only me and the prisoner were at home—I did not break open the door.
JOHN LAW . I am a linen-draper. The prisoner bought some things of me, and paid me with two half-crowns—I put them in the till with the rest—inquiry was made of me next morning—I produced all the money I had in the till—there were three half-crowns—this produced is one of them—I cannot say how many I might have received that day—I know I gave change for gold.
JOHN WALLIS ( policeman). I took the prisoner and told her she was charged with this—she said she knew nothing about it.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Baron Plait.
[trialAccount t18480821-2042]
2042. JAMES DOWLING feloniously blabbing James Scott, with intent to do him some grevious bodily harm.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES SCOTT . I live in High-street, Shadwell. On 18th July, I was in front of the Red Lion, Deptford-road, drinking with other people—I went into the back yard to make water, and saw the prisoner sitting with two females—in a minute or two a stone struck me on the head, I did not see who threw it—I was at a regular place, out of thefemales' sight—I went up to the prisoner and asked him what he did it for—he said, "I meant to pitch the stone into the second butt"—I said, "It is very strange you should throw it at me when I am a stranger"—I spoke quietly—he said, "If you don't like it you can have more of it," and wanted to fight—he made a blow at me—we had two rounds, and both fell—he pulled out a knife, opened it, showed it to me and said, "I will give you this"—an officer came and made him shut it up—
said, "Then, b----your eyes, I won't go," and bit me in the eye—Mitchell he put it into his pocket for a minute or two, and then pulled it out, and wanted to fight again—we had another round, with the knife shut in his hand—I knocked him down—we got up again—I was rather enraged, and knocked him down again—he got up on his knees, opened the knife, and stabbed me in the lower part of my belly—I went down the street, but could not find a doctor—I felt the blood trickling down—some one put me into a cart, and took me to the hospital—I was there nine days, and continued an out patient afterwards.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How many times did you knock him down? A. Twice, it was not three times—he said he was too weak a man to fight with me, and be must stab me if I attempted to strike him again, but he had given the first offence—he only said so once, and I did not strike him after—he called on the police to protect him—he said I had assaulted him, and the policeman said he would take him if he did not shut the knife up—we had four rounds, each terminated in his being knocked down—I was recognised at the hospital as a person to be charged with an assault—a warrant was taken out against me on the day this happened—I have been before the Magistrate for it, and my friends paid 5 l. fine for me.
COURT. Q. Did the prisoner tell the policeman he armed himself with a knife to protect himself? A. No—when he threatened to stab me, I said, "If you do I will knock you down"—there were two females telling him to go on—he stabbed me, and said, "There you are."
JOSEPH TAYLOR . I am an iron-moulder, of Duke's-place. I was at the side of the public-house, heard a noise, went into the back-yard, and saw Scott: knock the prisoner down—he got up, and had some severe words—Scott pitched into him again—they both fell, kicking each other—Scott went outside the gate, and then came back, and began abusing the prisoner—they came to high words—the prisoner said if he struck him again he would use his knife—it was in his hand, closed—a policeman came—the prisoner said, "You are a policeman, do your duty," and gave Scott in charge—the policeman said, "If I do my duty, I shall take you, for having a knife," and parted them, and went away—Scott turned back, and said, "Do you dare to take out a knife to me?"—he said, "I do not want to fight you, I am not able to fight you"—Scott knocked him down two or three rimes, and went towards him as he laid on the ground, and was going to strike him while down, and the prisoner put the knife into him—I consider he did it in his own defence.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18480821-2043]
2043. GEORGE BAKER , feloniously assaulting George Bird, and wounding him on the forehead, with intent to resist his lawful apprehension.
MR. CAARTEEN conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN POTTER ( policeman, V 118). On Sunday, 6th Aug., I was on duty in Battersea Fields—there was a fair there—I saw the prisoner there with a convicted thief—they went behind a gentleman, and he put his hand to the bottom of his pocket, but got nothing there, they then went up to where there was a weighing-machine, and a number of people round it—the prisoner put his hand into Bird's pocket, and took out this handkerchief ( produced)—I said, "Have you lost anything?"—he put his hand into bis pocket, and said, "I have lost my handkerchief"—I took the prisoner—he had one corner of the handkerchief in his hand—Bird claimed it, and went with us—on the way to the station we went along a bank in the fields, twelve or fourteen feet high—the prisoner said to Bird, "Pray, let me go"—he said, "No"—the prisoner
came to my assistance—the prisoner knocked him down, very nearly into the Thames—I and Bird caught hold of the prisoner—we struggled against the fence—he endeavoured to get us towards the Thames—he struck me several times in the face, and caught me in the privates—he said, "B----your eyes, I will give it you"—three or four times over he said if I would let him get up he would go quietly—I did so—he struck me again—I saw a soldier while I was struggling, named George Bird, the brother of the other man—I called him to my assistance—he came—we all three went down together—the prisoner was undermost—he said to George Bird, "B----your eyes, I will do something for you," or something to that effect, and threw this brick at him ( produced), and cut him over the eye with it—there was a heap of bricks—he laid with his side against them—Tupney came to our assistance—we got the prisoner to the station.
Prisoner. Q. Was any one near me? A. About twenty people—one was a convicted thief, named Scott—I did not say I could make a good job of it now, and I could give you three months—I did not take the handkerchief off the ground—I was in plain clothes—I did not pull your handkerchief till you were black in the face, or kick your head—the blood on you was from Bird's forehead.
CHARLES BIRD . I am a labourer, of 37, Milton-street, Vincent-square. I was at Battersea Fields, by a weighing-machine—Potter showed me'a handkerchief, which I identified—he took the prisoner—I went with them—he resisted by the river—my brother came to my assistance, and the prisoner gave him a blow on the forehead with a brick.
GEORGE BIRD . I am a private in the first batalion of Grenadier Guards. I was at Battersea Fields, and saw the struggle—Potter asked me to assist—I took hold of the prisoner by his collar and handkerchief—he got up and made a rush at Potter to strike him—there was a scuffle, and he fell on a heap of bricks—Potter allowed him to get up—the prisoner rushed at him again—we got him down—he threw one or two bricks—the last one struck me on the forehead—I was quite senseless for several minutes—the skin was broken—it bled a great deal—I went to the hospital, and am not yet well.
WILLIAM OWEN TUPNEY ( policeman, V 48). I was on duty—a gentleman spoke to me—I went, and saw the prisoner scuffling with Potter—George Bird was bleeding furiously—I laid hold of the prisoner—he said, "If you had not come up when you had, I should have split the whole five b----skulls, and then I should have got away"—there was a mob—I took him to the station.
FRANCIS CORNELIUS HUTHWAITE . I am surgeon to Bird's regiment. I examined his head—he had a contused wound on the forehead—the skin was broken—this brick would have done it—he is still in the hospital—any blunt body thrown with violence would cause a similar wound, but it would require violence.
Prisoner's Defence. The handkerchief was across my legs; I knew nothing about it; the policeman put his knuckles into my neck, and I struck him; he struck me, knocked me down, and knelt on me; the prosecutor struck me several times; I was bruised all over.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18480821-2044]
2044. GEORGE SHELL , unlawfully making a certain seditious speech and for attending an unlawful assembly.
MR. ATTORNEY-GENERAL, with MESSRS. WELSBY, BODKIN, and CLERK conducted the Prosecution.
WIILIAM COUNSELL . I live at Camberwell, and am a short-hand writer—on the evening of Friday, 28th July, I was present at a meeting at the Chartist Hall, Webber-street, Blackfriars'-road—I got there shortly before eight o'clock—I should say there were from 150 to 200 people present—the number increased afterwards to about 500—they appeared to be working men—a person named Bryson was elected chairman—I heard the defendant speak there—three persons had spoken before him, one was a person of the name of Keene—I took a short-hand note of Shell's speech—this is it—( reads —"Mr. Chairman and fellow-workingmen—Being called upon to come forward and propose the second resolution to be submitted to your notice this evening, I do so with feelings of great pleasure and satisfaction, because the opportunity which now presents itself leads me to embrace that opportunity for the purpose of expressing my opinion as to what I consider to be the policy of the English Government towards that unfortunate country, Ireland, as well as to express my determination and disapprobation of the same. The resolution I submit to you is as follows:—'Resolved, that this meeting do solemnly protest against the base and unconstitutional act of the British Legislature, as committed by them in the suspending of the Habeas Corpus Act in Ireland; and further, do hereby tender to Feargus O'Connor, Esq., Member of Parliament, and to those gentlemen who supported him, our best thanks for their manly and patriotic conduct on that occasion; and further, that we do hereby solemnly pledge ourselves, that we will render in the hour of trial our full aid and co-operation to our Irish brethren.' In making a few remarks upon the resolution I have submitted to you, you will at once perceive that the resolution which I have moved may fairly be divided into three points; first, condemnation; secondly, commendation; and thirdly, determination. In reference to the act of the Legislature in suspending the Habeas Corpus Act in Ireland, it may-perhaps be necessary, for a few moments, that we take a retrospective view of the proceedings of that Legislature in connection with Irish affairs. If we look back to history, and trace the proceedings from the time of the Union up to the present, we shall find that the policy of the English Government towards that unhappy country has been one systematic system of coercion. The Irish people at the time of the Union were promised equality with the English people; they were promised that they should have equal laws and equal justice with the English people. Now, Mr. Chairman, although I have not condemned the acts of the Legislature in connection with my own country, I have still to condemn, and more justly too, I think, the acts of the Legislature in connection with Ireland, whenever and by whomsoever an appeal has been made to the Legislature of this country for justice to the Irish people, it has always been met by a system of coercion entirely; the English people have been coerced as well, and more especially of late, than they formerly were. You will remember that the English people, as well as the Irish people, have wrongs which they justly complain of, and they complain most reasonably and most justly too, because, as men and Englishmen, and as the wealth-producers of this country, they consider they have a right to a fair representation in the House of Commons, which at present they are denied. Such is the case with Ireland; and until, as my friend who moved the first resolution observed, Ireland is fairly represented by the people and for the people, no good will ever accrue either to the one country or the other. We know, Mr. Chairman, that there have been fine spirits of late exhibited in the sister country, as well as in this country; men have sprung up who have declared to the people, and told them and taught them what they are as men,
what they should be as men, and how they may obtain that which they seek Mitchell, the Irish patriot, who has been condemned and transported as a felon for freely explaining to the Irish people what was their duty, what were their rights, and how they were to obtain them, has been sacrificed and made the victim, for promulgating those principles which I am quite glad to see at present are so prominent among the Irish people. Ireland has been taught by the Apostle Mitchell, and I rejoice to see that, although the Government has removed him, there are other individuals equally as patriotic as himself, who have nobly and manfully come forward and taken the people by the hand, and declared that they will to the death lead them on, if possible, to victory. Mr. Chairman, I rejoice that such has been the case; and I recommend to the Irish people to follow implicitly the commands of their leaders. I would call also upon the English people who desire liberty, who desire justice, and who desire equality, to stand by the Irish people in the hour of need. As soon as the Irish people are up, as soon as the insurrection in Ireland becomes general, (as I believe in the course of a very few hours it will become general), I trust that the people of England will rise to support them. Mr. Chairman and fellow working men, for whatever I say I care not, whatever may follow, I as an Englishman consider that on all occasions, and upon every occasion, when my conscience dictates to me that I have an opportunity of speaking to my fellow men in public or in private, I have an unbounded right to tell them what is the honest conviction of my mind; and if I suffer for so doing, I shall only be another added to the band of patriots who have been crushed; and if, working men, you do allow those men who do nobly come forward and tell you your rights, and how to obtain them, if you allow them to be picked away, if you allow them to be transported, to enjoy, I was going to say, but rather to be placed in a dungeon, there to suffer all the indignities which it is possible for a base, bloody, and brutal Government to inflict, you do not deserve the name of men, but you ought to be incarcerated yourselves, your families ought to starve around you, and you ought to go howling through the streets as a base and cowardly race of men. Gentlemen, I am not inclined to take up much of your time this evening. In what I say, however humble it may be, remember I am only a working man, and have not much time to spare to study to make use of fine grammatical expressions. I sincerely and honestly co-operate with the friends of liberty as much as those who have more time to spare and to devote to the cause. But, gentlemen, allow me to ask you if you are men, will ye act as men? (cries of "Yes.") If ye are called on to-night, to-morrow, or at any other time, do not show you are cowards, and flinch, but rally round the standard of liberty, defend it with your lives, and may God Almighty grant we may be victorious, that our enemies may be crushed, that they may hide their heads in the dungeons in the hulks, and that our honest patriots may be restored to us, and that they may take their places is my sincere wish."
Q. In what manner was this speech received by the meeting? A. With applause at various parts of it—the resolution Mr. Keene moved was as follows:—"Resolved, that this meeting views with feelings of distrust and alarm the measure adopted by her Majesty's Government, and sanctioned by the Imperial Parliament, for the suppression of public opinion in Ireland, as tending to the final severance of all amicable relations between the two countries; and this meeting is further of opinion, that the Irish people are fully entitled to a free and independent Parliament, and therefore resolves to render to their patriotic brethren in Ireland their cordial sympathy."
before the defendant spoke he was introduced by the chairman in the meeting as the secretary—after Shell's speech, some Irish, a deputation of the Mitchell Club, came in—I think Shell was there then, but I cannot be positive.
Prisoner. Q. When I said, "If you are men, will you act as men?" will you swear positively the answer gicen was "Yes?" A. Yes, I have it "yes" put down at the time.
Court. Q. Did you observe whether the prisoner read the resolution, or gave it? A. It was read as if it had been previously prepared.
WILLIAM HENRY MAINWARING ( policeman, L 86). I apprehended the prisoner on 2nd Aug. on a warrant, as he was coming out of the Chartist meeting in Webber-street—I afterwards went to where he lived, No. 2, Princes-place, Princes-street, London-road, and found this book and paper ( produced) there—I took them from a room pointed out to me by Mr. Lord.
GEORGE LORD. I live at 2, Princes-place, London-road—the defendant rented a room there—when Mainwaring came there on 2nd Aug. I accompanied him to the room where the defendant lodged, and there saw him find the book and paper produced—( read —"Mr. Side, I am instructed to request the favour of your attendance at a meeting of the London secretaries, to be held on Sunday morning, at ten o'clock; be prepared to report the number of the enrolled members, and the general state of the district. Signed, on behalf of the Executive, L. M. Kid, John-street Institution, July 25, 1848. Make the bill enclosed public as possible."—" John-street, July 25, 1848,—Mr. Charles Keene,—I am instructed to request you to be present at the meeting of the London secretaries, to be held in the Assembly-rooms, Dean-street on Sunday next, at ten o'clock, a.m. Be prepared to report on the state of the district, and give a return of the enrolled members, &c. Signed, on behalf of the Executive, SAML. KIDD. ")—in the book was written in pencil, "Proposed by Mr. Cufiy,—That each district send four men to Cartwright's, to arrange a plan of communication."
MR. RICHARD KEMMIS . I produce from the office of the Clerk of the Crown of Dublin a copy of the record of he conviction of John Mitchell—I examined it with the original—it is correct— The Attorney-General here stated that the indictment against Mitchell was for printing and publishing certain articles in the "United Irishman," and read the following passages,—" Mob law itself in Ireland is far better than Government law; that well-ordered and civilized system, that slays its millions of human beings within the year. I tell you, that rather than endure one other year of British domination, I would take a provisional government, selected out of the men that are bellowing out there in the streets" (loud cheers)—"Or can I condemn the alternative put by Mr. Meagher, who says, 'When the last constitutional appeal shall be made, and shall fail, then up with the barricades, and invoke the God of battles.' Can I repudiate this, who bold that constitutional appeals are long since closed against us, and that we have even now no resource, except when we have the means and the pluck to do it. The barricades and the God of battles' No; all the seditions and treasons of these gentlemen I adopt and accept, and I ask for more. Whatever has been done or said by the most disaffected persons in all Ireland against the existence of the party which calls itself the Government, nothing can go too far for me. Whatever public treasons there are in this land, I have stomach for them all.'—"But while we meet the enemy in the Queen's Bench, we have a right to call upon you to sustain us by a firm and universal avowal of your opinion—upon the
constituents of Smith O'Brien especially devolves this duty—while the British Parliament calls his exertions treason and felony, it is for his constituents to declare, that in all this treason and felony he is doing his duty by them; and more than this, it is your duty to prepare systematically to sustain him, if it come to that, in arms. May I presume to address the women of Limerick? It is the first time I have ever been in the presence of the daughters of those heroines who held the breach against King "William; and they will understand me when I say, that no Irishwoman ought so much as to speak to a man who has not provided himself with arms. No lady is too delicate for the culinary operation of casting bullets; no hand is too white to make up cartridges; and I hope, if it be needful to come to the last resort, that the citizens of Limerick, male and female, will not disgrace their paternal and maternal ancestors"——"Now the fact is, the editor of the United Irishman, " meaning Mr. Mitchell, "is no hero at all, and never said he was: he has only endeavoured to persuade his countrymen that they will never gain their liberties except by fighting for them; and that the only arguments the British Government will understand are the points of pikes, that's all; and he continues to preach this saving doctrine, and will continue so to do, until a considerable number of his countrymen agree with him, and then he hopes to aid in enforcing the arguments practically, that's all. As for the sneaking away of Mr. Mitchell, or Mr. Meagher, or any of them, under the protection of the police, it is merely an untruth; the writer in the Times who wrote it, and the editor of Saunders and the Mail, who we find have copied it, know it to be an untruth."——"How are we to meet that day? In arms, my countrymen, in arms! Thus, and not otherwise, have ever nations of men sprung to liberty and power: but why do I reason thus with you, Irishmen cf Ulster, who never have denied the noble creed and sentiment of manhood? You have not been schooled for forty years in the fatal cant of moral force; you have not been utterly debauched and emasculated by the clap-trap platiudes of public meetings, and the empty glare of imposing demonstrations. You have not yet learned the litany of slaves, and the whine of beaten hounds, and the way to die a coward's death. No; let once the great day of your country's destiny seize on you my townsmen, and the way will be as plain before you as a pike-staff twelve feet long."
MICHAEL HAYDON ( City policeman, 21). I know Cartwright's coffee-shop in Whitecross-street—I know that meetings of Chartists have been held there—I was present at this Court last session, and heard the evidence that was given with respect to a tumult at that place on 29th May—I was not present at it.
THOMAS COPE . I am the publisher of the Times newspaper—I have here the file-copy of the second edition of the Times of 27th July, 184S—it contains a report of a telegraphic dispatch of the reported rising in Ireland—( reads —"Ireland, Thursday morning. In the absence of any report from our correspondent, we publish, without vouching for its accuracy, the following intelligence, just received from the Electric Telegraph Company:—Liverpool, Thursday morning. The following intelligence was received by special engine last evening by the editor of the Dublin Post:—'The whole of the south of Ireland is in rebellion, the station at Thurles is on fire, the rails for several miles torn up, and the mob's intention is to detain the engines as they arrive at Clonmel. The fighting is dreadful. The people arrived in masses. The Dublin Club leaders were there. The troops were completely overpowered; many refused to act. The military at Carrick have shown disaffection, and have been driven back, and their quarters fired. At Kilkenny the contest is proceeding,
and here too the mob are said to have been successful. No news from Waterford or Cork.' "
Prisoner's Defence. Having been arraigned at the bar of this Court to-day to answer to a charge preferred against me by the Gentlemen of the Government, it is not my intention to follow them through all the mazes and intricacies of the law, for I believe the present existing laws to be so complicated and confused, as to pass my humble powers of conception; but although I stand at the bar of this Court to-day, I trust that no man in this Court will for a moment suppose that I stand here accused of any crime, or any act of dishonesty that I may have committed against my fellow-men; no, not withstanding all the difficulties that I feel surround me this day, it is a consolation to me to know, that in standing here my honour and integrity as a man, are not impeached; I am here merely for differing from the Government in opinion; for that, and that alone, and in support of that assertion, allow me to draw your attention to the materials out of which this prosecution has been framed. You have heard that on the evening of the 28th July, a meeting was held at the Chartist-hall, Webber-street, and that I at that meeting delivered a speech which has been termed seditious. Was there ever a more diabolical, a more fabricated statement urged on the face of an indictment than that upon which I have this day been accused? I have been accused of having maliciously and wilfully excited her Majesty's subjects to rebellion, and so forth. Now I contend that the remarks I made use of at that meeting are supportable by historical facts. You have heard from the evidence of Wm. Counsel, the Government reporter, that I felt a pleasure and satisfaction in expressing my opinion of the policy of the British Government towards Ireland, and in moving the resolution condemnatory of the base and unconstitutional acts of the British Legislature I stated that it was necessary to refer to history to support the assertion which I then made, and on referring to history, what do we find? why, that up to the period of 1782, Ireland was free from English legislative thraldom, and it was not till that date that the Act 6 Geo. III. passed, which claimed and exercised the power of British legislation over Ireland. Now the mere passing of that Act, even of itself, admits the preexistence of freedom, and leads us to inquire how a nation, that long before 1300 possessed its own legislature, came to give up to a foreign power the right of self-government. What do we find? Why, that the Irish parliament, backed by the Irish people, exerted this power of independence in legislation for their own country. But passing over that period, and coming down to the time of the American war, when that people revolted against England for attempting to trample on their legislative rights, and insisted upon perfect legislative independence with such an ardour and force of arms, that England became alarmed and proposed a final adjustment, which was entered into. But England never yet preserved or performed a treaty with Ireland; and thus we perceive one encroachment after another, until we come down to the period of 1800, when the Act of Union was carried by the most diabolical and contemptible means that was ever resorted to for a class legislation; which atrocious acts of plunder has at last plunged the country into all the horrors of insurrection. Such has been the policy of the English Government towards Ireland. Will you tell the world, by your verdict this day, that it is sedition for a man to sympathise with Ireland's wrongs? To tell them that they have a right to use any constitutional means which they have in their power to remove the tyrants from power who have brought them to death's door by heir acts of tyranny? Will you tell the world, by your verdict, that it is sedition to tell your countrymen to organize and oppose the same tyrants
who are trying to reduce you to the same depths of misery and degradation? Will you tell the world that it is sedition for a man to try to unite Englishmen and Irishmen together? Gentlemen, I hope better things of you; are: are men, I presume, who are moving in what are called the middle circles of society, some of whom, I doubt not, have sons who, when brought to years of maturity, will have to grapple and struggle with the affairs of business; think seriously of the ruin and misery which the atrocious policy of the Government is entailing upon you by seeking to destroy thac spirit of justice and growing intelligence which the working classes are determined to enjoy, and to remove the fruits of those who have so long laboured for the working classes, just as they are beginning to make their appearance. But the working classes are beginning to understand that they are the wealth-producers, and are entitled to receive a proportionate share of enjoyment, and they are determined to meet from time to time to consider what steps are likely to promote their cause, and to discuss and promote tbeir principles and if the noble Lord at the head of the Government supposes that by employing a few thousand policemen to break the heads of honest and hard-working men, and by employing spies to procure convictions, through the means of prejudiced juries, he will ultimately find himself mistaken. The avidity with which the great majority of the people of this country have adopted the principles of the people's charter, seems greatly to have frightened the noble Lord at the head of the Government; and no wonder, when the eyes of the people being to open, and they begin to inquire how the taxes have become so enormously increased, and when they see nothing but bankruptcy and ruin staring then in the face. This has led a great many men to desire a speedy change in public affairs, and to inquire how this state of things has been brought about, The noble lord has said that the disaffection of the Chartists has been to such an unparalleled extent, as has obliged him to increase the rates in order to support the men who had come forward in order to put down that disaffection; but this lame story has only led the people to make further inquiries, and to adopt such measures as to bring about a better state of things, and the means they had decided upon are the principles of the people's charter. Do you know those principles, Gentlemen?—if not, allow me to refer you to that excellent little pamphlet written by Dr. M'Douall, wherein be states what they are, and what they want. I believe that the principles of the people's charter contain one of the best political creeds that was ever submitted. Its points are easy of conception, and its principles are just. It does not, as some assert, wish to destroy one class of society to raise up another, nothing of the sort; but it does wish to give to all a just political equality. It seeks to give to every man of sound mind, untainted by crime, a voice in the election of the representatives of the people. It seeks to destroy a property qualification, for the purpose of giving men of talent, without pecuniary resources, an opportunity of obtaining a seat in Parliament. It seeks to prevent that disgraceful intimidation and bribery which is carried on under the present system. It seeks to shorten the duration of Parliaments, to keep the representatives of the people honest. It seeks, as nearly as possible, equal electoral districts, so as to give as fair a representation of the country at large as can possibly be obtained. These are some of the leading principles of the charter; and for calling upon my fellow-countrymen to organise, never to cease their agitation, but to avail themselves of every opportunity to forward their cause, I have been charged with all the foul and malignant falsehoods contained in the indictment; and for this the learned counsel wishes to obtain conviction. I feel assured, Gentlemen, that I need not remind you of the
good conduct and forbearance of the people on 10th April, when the Government mustered all their forces, and banded all their troops, to prevent the people from getting free access to Parliament. I feel assured that you are already convinced that the contents of the indictment are nothing more than a tissue of falsehoods, and that this is only intended for the purpose of putting down public opinion—of destroying the right of public meeting, and making victims of honest men. Do not lend yourselves as the tool of such a Government; but let your manly and consistent verdict this day tell the tyrants of misrule and oppression, that Englishmen shall inherit the ancient rights of their forefathers, and the gratitude of a grateful people will reverence your memories in ages yet to come.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Two Years, fined 10 l., and to enter into his can recognizances in 100 l., and to find two sureties of 50 l. each, to keep the peace for five years.
[trialAccount t18480821-2045]
2045. JAMES MAXWELL BRYSON was indicted for a like offence.
The ATTORNEY-GENERAL, MESSRS. WELSBY, BODKIN, and CLERK conducted the Prosecution.
FREDERICK BOND HUGHES . I am a short-hand writer. On Friday evening, 28th July, I was present at a meeting at the Chartist-hall, Webber-street, Blackfriars-road—I got there a few minutes before eight—about 150 persons were then assembled—the number afterwards increased to the extent of between four and five hundred—they consisted of mechanics—the defendant presided as chairman—he was not in the chair before I got into the room, but a few minutes afterwards, as the numbers gradually increased, the chairman and other parties appeared on the platform—he took the chair without being moved in—he addressed the meeting in the first instance—I took a note of his speech, and have it here—( reads)—"Ladies and Gentlemen, we have met this evening to give utterance to sentiments of sympathy in behalf of a people who have suffered the first shock of the tyrant's vengeance. Whether the intelligence that was received, and generally circulated yesterday, is correct or incorrect it still requires some time to elucidate; but it is very evident that the excuse, and the lame story told by Sir George Grey last night in the House of Commons, tallies very ill with what appears this morning in the papers. A newspaper when it receives intelligence from its correspondent at a distance, know whether to place confidence in the man or not; and it is not likely that so many correspondents would put their heads together to send one and the same story to this country by express, and then the next day contradict every word of it. They must. have stated, the correspondents themselves must have acknowledged, this morning, that they were gross liars, and it is not very likely that they are going to do that for their employers; or else they must have stated that they fell asleep from over fatigue, and dreamt all about it. However it is very evident something is going on in Ireland; what it is we do not exactly know, but it is quite evident this Government is afraid of what is going on in Ireland—(cries of "let us go on here, now is the time)—Gentlemen, if I am your chairman this evening, I hope you will keep order, and if any gentleman makes use of expressions in the body of the Hall, I hope none of you will pay any attention to him, for depend upon it that Gentleman has not any good intention towards your cause, for it is the way in which all Government spies attempt to get up a disturbance in public meetings; now be upon your guard, I beg of you, for it is quite evident they are sent on purpose to do such things—(a voice, "they ought to be kicked out")—if you are not satisfied with this meeting, and if you have got anything to say, I shall be
happy if you will come on the platform and say it, and all the company besides. I beg of you to have a little patience; do not mind the interrupttion, pay no attention to it, that is the best way to get over it—there can be little doubt, as I was stating to you, there must be something doing in Ireland of an extraordinary nature, because even in this day's paper, in the organ of Government, the Times, it mentions the fact that 400 warrants have been issued against the confederate leaders, by the Lord Lieutenant, in one day; that does not look amicable, or as if things were going on peaceably and quietly. It is stated how many forges were ringing with the manufacture of pikes, in defiance of the police and the authorities. Troops have been leaving every morning from London and other parts to go to that unhappy country, as I must call it. But nevertheless, it is evident that whatever has been done, they have stood the shock very nobly, if shock it has been. There can be very little doubt the sword is drawn and the bullet is flying on the other side of the channel—(Loud cheers)—our rulers may attempt to disguise the fact, but the truth will ultimately come out. There can be no question about it at the present moment, that we are in a very precarious situation: it is for us, however, to remain quiet, and not commit ourselves in any way; and I trust any gentleman who comes up here to say a word this evening, will be very careful of his language, because a very good authority; and we, the Chartists, have great confidence in his authority on law; informed me to-day that this was, according to the new Act, a seditious meeting. However, Gentlemen, I have not withdrawn from what I promised the learned counsel, and that was to take the chair this evening, although he informed me it was seditious; but I trust none of you will allow me to answer for your crimes, it is quite enough if I answer for my own, and all the words upon this Bill I hold in my hand, and all the expressions I have given utterance to I have no objection to go into a Court of Justice and defend, without the aid of Counsel. I do not want any attorney to pick out a flaw in the indictment, or any forensic oratory to get me off; I stand by the principles mentioned in the Bill; I stand by the words I have used, but I hope and trust you will be careful in what you say, for I am answerable for everything you say this evening, the same as if I spoke it myself. With these remarks, Gentlemen, as there are several resolutions to come before this meeting, I trust none of you will overstrain your language; no doubt many of you are warm and enthusiastic in it, and perhaps might feel the spirit rising as it did in Douglas, and you might perhaps say you 'longed to follow to the field some warlike chief, but you must not say that here. With respect to that unfortunate country, Ireland, there can be no doubt also that our Government has sent some of the very harshest—to give it the mildest term—the harshest of their Generals. The last new importation, the oriental slaughterman has been sent over; that gentleman has been sent over, no doubt, for the purpose of slaughtering our Irish brethren, doing it in a Sikh fashion; but it is to be hoped that he will find even a tougher foe than the Sikh was. That General, not to call him by a more harsh name, that human butcher, was in the habit of tying his red bandanna to his bloody sword, and waving it in the air as a signal telegraph to his troops to go in and cut down women, men, and children who came in reach of the weapon. Is he gone to Ireland to do the same thing? He is gone, but whether for that purpose or for a more amicable one we have not the opportunity of knowing. With these few remarks, Gentlemen, I will sit down, and call upon your old friend—and I also beg to state, before I sit down, that it may create no confusion hereafter, that as we are only working people who have got up this meeting—you know a meeting of this
description cannot be got up without expense—and therefore there will be a collection this evening for the purpose of defraying the expense. I hope we shall hare many such numerous meetings as we have now, and as peaceable as the last time we met in this Hall. With these remarks I will sit down, and call upon your old friend and warden, Mr. Keene, to propose the first resolution; and he will also read you a letter from an individual you will be happy to hear would have attended this evening, were it possible; and also, I may state that even the Government hirelings of this country are beginning to use the victims at present in their possession rather harshly; although the Defence-fund has paid for the maintenance of Mr. Vernon in prison, to exempt him from labour, nevertheless he has been forced to pick oakum until his fingers were blistered. This is no flying report; I have had it to-day from the lips of Mrs. Vernon herself; and also I have seen a letter from Dr. M'Douall, which you will soon have the miserable pleasure of reading tomorrow in the 'Star.' That gentleman was thrust into a cell through which the hot water pipes passed, for the purpose of conveying hot water to other apartments of the prison, and there they kept him stewing, I cannot give it any other name; for hoars, until he refused to complain, because it only gratified them; and this gentleman was obliged to take off his coat, waistcoat, and trowsers, and stand up in a corner of the cell to keep life, soul, and body together—(Loud cries of shame). That is the state of your Government, and the resolution therefore calls upon you loudly to sympathize, particularly with your Irish brethren, who no doubt are suffering similar acts of outrage and violence."
Q. How was that speech received? A. With cheers—a person named Keene then spoke—in the course of his speech he said: "Mitchell saw the misery his country was suffering under; Mitchell knew he must be a sacrifice, but he was willing to be a sacrifice, if by—that sacrifice he could rouse his countrymen to a perfect knowledge of what their rights were"—(three cheers for Mitchell were then given)—the resolution that was proposed by Mr. Keene was, "Resolved, that this meeting views with feeling of distrust and alarm the measure adopted by Her Majesty's Government, and sanctioned by the Imperial Parliament, for the repression of public opinion in Ireland, as tending to the final severance of all amicable relations between the two countries; and this meeting is further of opinion that the Irish people are fully entitled to a free and independent parliament, and therefore resolves to render to their patriotic brethren in Ireland their cordial sympathy." After Keene, a person named Pedley spoke—the following is a passage in his speech: "I believe, Mr. Chairman, the fact is this, that Ireland is in arms; that they are fighting there; I trust they will speedily gain a victory, and God speed them in their cause!"—that was received with cheers—the resolution was then put by the defendant to the meeting, and carried unanimously—Shell then spoke—I took his speech—Mr. Counsell also took it—we each took the meeting throughout—Shell proposed the second resolution—it was this: "Resolved, that this meeting do solemnly protest against the base and unconstitutional act of the British Legislature, as committed by them in the suspendin? of the Habeas Corpus Act in Ireland; and, further, do hereby tender to Feargus O'Connor, Esq., M. P., and to those gentlemen who supported him, our best thanks for their manly and patriotic conduct on that occasion; and, further, that we do hereby solemnly pledge ourselves that we will render in the hour of trial our full aid and co-operation to our Irish brethren"—I did not take that part where the defendant introduced Mr. Rooney to second a resolution—I have Rooney's speech—he said, "Mr. Chairman, Englishmen, and Irishmen too, I don't know that I am in my place to-night in seconding this resolution;
I only come among you as a visitor, but I wish to ask you one or two questions, and I take the opportunity of seconding the resolution to ask to ask you—I wish to know, has organization done its duty among you?—are you satisfied, Englishmen, that the troops of Britain are sent to slaughter your fellowmen in Ireland I Are you satisfied? We are not satisfied, and we are come to the determination, and let the reporter hear me, that sooner than any shall leave London for Ireland, they must leave it across our corpses (cheers); I made a vow a month ago that I would never say anything at a meeting again; but you must pardon me to-night, for the future nothing but action is required for men; I beg to second the resolution"—there were some words at the conclusion which escaped me—Mr. Counsell's note is fuller than mine—after that the defendant spoke again—he said, "I rise for the purpose of answering the two questions Mr. Rooney has required to have answered. One was, whether we were organized or not? You are aware we are organized, and that all the acknowledged Chartists belonging to this Hall are organized, and under the proper officers; but every individual who comes into this Hall is not under organization, therefore we have no control over them. The other question was, whether you were satisfied with the present abuse towards Ireland.? '(cries of 'No!') and whether you would co-operate, and whether you are willing, that is, the organized part of you, because the others are of no use to any party, whether those who belong to the organization are willing to cooperate with the confederates (cries of 'Yes! ') It bas been said some time ago, that the confederates would not come over to us, and that we must go over to them; but this evening is a contradiction to that statement, for the confederates have come to you, and they are willing to co-operate; and I trust Mr. Rooney, when he returns to his club, will inform them that the organized Chartists of the South Lambeth locality, are willing to go hand in hand with any scheme that the confederates have for the amelioration of Ireland"—immediately after that Rooney said, "I beg to tender the best gratitude of the John Mitchell Club, for your sympathy with their unfortunate countryman, and I hope"—there was an interruption there—something was said about a nod being as good as a wink to a blind horse, but I did not write it down—Mr. Counsell has it.
Prisoner. Q. You have mentioned that I took the chair without any ceremony? A. I did not hear you proposed into the chair—you seemed to me to take it as a matter of course, as if there had been some previous arrangement—no one interrupted me in taking my report—I asked for accommodation as soon as I entered the room, and stated the purpose for which I came—I took, the whole of the meeting verbatim, not merely the heads—there was so much confusion, that if any proposition was made for you to take the chair, I did not take it.
WILLIAM COUNSELL . I attended this meeting in Webber-street on 28th July—I took the speech made by Shell, in proposing the second resolurion—( reading it, as in page 667)—the resolution moved by that speech was put by the chairman to the meeting, and carried—I took a note of what Rooney said after his two questions had been answered by the chairman—he said, "Gentlemen, I beg to tender you the best gratitude of the John Mitchell Clab. for your sympathy with my unfortunate countrymen; and I hope" (a voice, "That will do; a wink is as good as a nod to a blind horse.")
JOHN BYWATER ( policeman, L 116). I apprehended the prisoner on 2nd Aug., upon a warrant, in Webber-street, where the Chartist meeting was—I searched him at the station, and found on him this paper ( produced)" —I knew that he resided at 25, James-street, Lower-marsh, Lambeth—I went here, and there found this sword, this file or pike this book, some papas.
and some cards ( producing them: these being read, one was a copy of a resolution—" "Resolved, that this meeting pledge themselves to give countenance and support to the brave patriots of Ireland, who have so nobly opposed the enemies of freedom in their career of unjustifiable bloodshed." One card was as follows —"South London Life and Property Protection Society. Name, James Bryson; entered, May 12, 1848; signed, James Bryson, Secretary pro. tem." Another card referred to the entry of the defendant as a member of the National Charter Association; and the papers related to the formation and rules of The Life and Property Protection Society," which was to consist of an unlimited number of members, above the age of eighteen, each to be armed with a musket or pike, to be paid for by instalments; and the following resolution —"That the householders, and inhabitants of the southern parts of the metropolis, agree to enrol themselves as a Life and Property Protection Society, for the fallowing reasons:—to calm the alarm, and afford a speedy and effective protection to the lives and property of the inhabitants; to save the expense and trouble of summoning and swearing in special constables; to show that the people are not desirous of assaulting the persons, or injuring the property of the middle classes; to maintain peace, law, and order at public meetings; to legally arm themselves, at their own expense, and he ready to act with the authorities whenever the lives and property of the public are in danger:" on the back was written, "Carried.")
Prisoner. Q. You mentioned at Bow-street that you found these articles in a room over the washhouse? A. Yes—I was never in the washhouse, I supposed it to be one—I am not a mechanic—I believe I called this instrument a dagger, when I was first examined—I do not know whether I called it a pike—I have seen a burnisher—this is not like any burnisher I have ever seen—I should not imagine it to be one.
( Mr. Thomas Cope read the paragraph from " The Times," as in page 670; the extracts from the record of the conviction of John Mitchell were also read.) Prisoner's Defence. It is not my intention to follow the learned gentleman through all the mazes of the law—first, because I conceive that the late stringent enactments that have been passed for the purpose of crushing those who have embraced the same political opinions which I this day stand here to advocate, are no doubt sufficiently explicit in themselves, to give him all the advantage which a prosecutor requires to enable him to procure a conviction; and secondly, it would be utterly out of my power to do so—being immured in a gaol, I was of course obliged to submit to the discipline of the place; and although I was supplied with materials for writing, and such purposes, yet I was denied the use of those books "which I considered were essential for my defence; this may seem rather strange, nevertheles it is to fact, and this restriction was carried to such an extent, that not only were political pamphlets refused me, but even a sight of the Penny Cyclopœdia, containing a definition of the word "Sedition," had the chaplain's veto affixed to it; and further, I could obtain no knowledge of the specific nature of the charge against me until Saturday, when I heard it very imperfectly in this Court, when I was called up to plead; but whether this is an arrangement for the distinction of political and criminal offenders, or whether it is an established rule of all the prisons in the kingdom, I am not sufficiently conversant with criminal jurisprudence and prison discipline to determine; but I trust you will at once perceive, that labouring under such disadvantages, it places me in a position not at all calculated to meet the charge, particularly when that charge has been preferred by a gentleman who stands at the top of his profession. But with all these disadvantages, I do not despair of being
able to show you that the part I took in the political sympathy with my brother democrats was nothing more than a justifiable right, inherited by all freemen; nor are you to suppose, because I stand before you unassisted by counsel, that I do so for the purpose of creating sympathy in my behalf I do it from a conviction that any one of you, similarly situated, and having the least regard for your word and honour, would have acted the same; neither are you to suppose that my political associates have abandoned me to my fate; on the contrary, I was no sooner arrested than professional aid was tended and refused, on the ground that however learned the advocate might be or however conversant with the intricacies of his profession, conviction would follow conviction, until once that class of society out of which juries are composed become convinced that to coerce the working classes is but the steppin-stone to their own political and social degradation. I have no doubt that most of you have presided at a public meeting during some period of your life, and, therefore, you are aware that some warmth of language is required on the part of the chairman, that the audience may be made acquainted with the nature of the motives for calling them together; and if I, on the 28th ult., while acting as chairman, gave utterance to language contrary to law, it was nevertheless dictated by views of humanity. We had received the day before, by public intelligence, a brief account of Irish disturbances; and as that people were struggling for political freedom, withheld from them by the same power that kept us in political thraldom, it was but natural we should tender our sympathies at such a crisis, and particularly when our grievances were so closely allied; and I do not think there was anything dangerous in the reply I made to Mr. Rooney, that we were willing to co-operate for the amelioration of the Irish people; common courtery required me to say as much, seeing that that gentleman headed a deputation of fifty members from the Mitchell Club; and certainly, had I returned a less favourable answer, there can be little doubt that a disturbance would have been the consequence, because there were a great number of policemen in disguise, in the room at the time, and they had made several attempts to disturb the meeting during the evening, and a greater number outside fully armed and ready to act at a moment's notice, fully determined to break up the meeting if possible prematurely. I was aware of their intention both inside and outside the room, for I had just received a note from one of those sent to watch their proceedings, telling me to endeavour to keep order if possibly could, because, said he, 'the butchers outside," this is the policemen, I do not disguise the fact, "are evidently bent on: slaughter, and all they want is a pretext to begin." That the police are capable of doing such things you are all very well aware, from the late investigation into the Bishop Bonner's-field police onslaught. It appears, however, that I conducted the meeting to its close without the least approximation to disorder. Then as for putting the public into great alarm, as the learned gentleman has stated, with respect to the Life and Property Protection Society, I can only tell him that such things are very common, and I should suppose he had seen similar societies in this country and other parts of Europe: it is certainly called a Life and Property Protection Society, and that was by the advice of counsel, and also by the advice of the Mayor of Nottingham, and the society founded in the city of Nottingham was approved of by the Mayor of that city; and our rules are the same. It has nothing to do with the Chartist Association, and in fact that society does not exist at the present. time in any locality that I am aware of, because it was assumed that the Chartist Association was sufficient to answer all the purposes. As for the
organization that is merely an arrangement, as to the internalmanagement of the society, and the documents referring to it, can be purchased at any of the political booksellers for a halfpenny. With respect to Mr. Vernon and D M'Douall, if I had been allowed to have the documents in I could have shown you the very words I read in my speech—or I might have sent for Mrs. Vernon, who no doubt could prove that her husband has sent word to that effect; for she stated in the presence of several other people besides myself, that he had been compelled to pick oakum, and that Feargus O'Connor had been sent to about it. As for Mr. Keene's speech, I can see notthing that very material against me; the fact is, he had had intelligence which is confirmed by another gentleman, Mr. Cope, who belongs to the Times, that Ireland was in a state of disturbance; and surely it was no great harm to repeat at a public meeting, that which appeared in the newspapers. As for the witness Bywater, perhaps there is nothing very objectionable in his evidence, he found the trunk containing these papers unlocked; many of them are not my writing, nor has there been any proof that they are the minutes of any particulars produced at any meeting. With respect to the sympathy we held with the people of Ireland, it is quite clear they were in a state of disturbance at the time I presided at this meeting: and are we on this side of the channel to be deprived of the rights of humanity, and be come amenable to the laws for sympathizing with those on the other side? for who among you, if he has not drained his veins of the milk of human kindness, would not sympathize with an oppressed people, standing on the threshold of civil war, and unable to say how soon the same horrors might surround your own dwellings? Does it not make your blood run cold, when you think that the maladministration of our internal affairs has driven the people to such a course, leaving them no other choice than open insurrection or unregretted starvation? But do not imagine that the coercive measures lately enacted were framed for the purpose of suppressing the spirit of freedom that is rising in the bosom of the honest mechanic will be laid aside when a few convictions have been obtained; on the contrary, when you have occasion to complain of ministerial oppression, and I do not think that many of you will say that the present Cabinet are doing that which is best calculated to promote contentment and prosperity in the country, that moment will you become subject to the same persecution, and those Acts of Parliament that have been framed to suppress us, will be unscrupulously used for your oppression; you may read a lesson of your future fate in the subterfuge which the present ministry resorted to on 10th April last, when the working classes of the metropolis attempted to exercise what has never been disputed to be a constitutional right,—the right of meeting and going in procession with the petition of their grievances to the Legislature. But, though they were determined to make no concessions, they were aware that there were sample reasons for complaining, and they felt no hesitation in raking up an obsolete act,—an act that had been consigned to oblivion for more than a century and a half, and one that had only been passed to prevent a debauched monarch from being interrupted in his royal revelries by the con gratulations of his loyal subjects;—I allude to the act of Charles II., which suppressed a constitutional right possessed by the people at that period: what are you to expect from such men as these, when you will no longer allow them to rob you with impunity? do you imagine that if Lord John Russell succeeds in suppressing the present discontent among the working classes, which is very doubtful, that he will next Session withdraw his bill for an additional income-tax, merely because it is not consonant with your
wishes, or will he consult the necessity of ministerial extravagance and bring you to compliance by statutory and military coercion—every thinking man in this country follows some political creed, the principles of which either accord with his pecuniary interest or coincide with his conscientious belief; and I have no hesitation in saying that I believe the Charter to be the best political creed that has been brought forward for the consideration of all classes. Its points are easy of comprehension, and its principles are honest. It does not offer concessions for one class of men, and refuse them to another; nor docs it involve the ruin of one set of men, as the necessary basis upon which another set are to flourish. Can there be any sedition in demanding for every man of sane mind, untainted with crime, and at the age of maturity, a voice in the election of the national representatives? Can it be sedition to demand security for that voice? Is it seditious to require the people's representatives to give an account of their delegation at shorter periods than they have hitherto done? or will it appear seditious to demand that a man of talent, without property, should have an equal right to a scat in the people's parliament which the man with property, but without talent, now possesses? Is it seditious to require that that man shall be remunerated for his labour? Or can you call it sedition to demand that each division of the kingdom be as nearly as possible equally represented? Yet such, Gentlemen, is the sum total of all the points of the Charter—the meaning; of the principles of this new philosophy, which has frightened the Sovereign, alarmed the Ministry, maddened the aristocracy, and distracted the middle classes, but now reigns paramount in the minds of the people. Depend upon it, Gentlemen, when the people understand and adopt a line of policy, neither scaffolds, nor dungeons, nor shackles, will arrest their onward career; nor will unprincipled oppression annihilate their power. You may do as Castlereagh did, thirty years ago, with the Radicals in the west of Scotland, when he emptied the gaols and bridewells of their criminal inmates, and set the felon free, that he might make room for honest Reformers: and although he brought some to the block, and sent some few into exile, yet radicalism prospered, and at this day stands high in the estimation of political reformers.
GUILTY . Aged. 38.— Confined Two Years; fined 20 l.; to enter into his own recognizances in 100 l., and to find two sureties of 50 l. each to keep the peace for five years .

2046. WILLIAM SMITH and ELIZABETH BROWN , burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Joseph Eden, and stealing 2 shirts, 1 pair of stockings, and 1 handkerchief, value 5 s. 6 d.; his goods.—2nd COUNT, for receiving the same.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH EDEN . I am the wife of Joseph Eden, of Richmond. On the evening of 17th July I was the last person up—I saw all the fastening secure about twelve o'clock—next morning I came down about seven—I was the first person up, and found the house in very great confusion—the kitchen window was open, and a pane of glass broken, sufficiently large for a person to put their hand through and undo the window—I missed a pair of my son's boots, two shirts, a pair of stockings, a blue and white cotton handkerchief, and a tin box, containing about 18 d. or 2 s. in money—they are my husband's and son's property—I had seen them safe the previous night—the shirts had been washed, and were wet—I know the stockings by my own darning, and the shirts are marked by me, "J. E.," my husband's initials—I know the handkerchief by the pattern, and its being a little torn.
JOHN PAYNE ( policeman, T 252). About four o'clock, on the afternoon of 18th July, I was on duty at Old Brentford, which is about three miles from Richmond and saw Brown—I had received information, and asked her what she had got in her bundle—she said, "Two shirts"—I asked whether they were her own—she said, no, they were her brother's—I asked where her brother was—she said, at the public-house (pointing to the One Tun); that she was going to sell the shirts for her brother—I went to the One Tun, and found no one there—I asked what the shirts were marked with—she said, "Red"—I asked what the initials were—she said she could not tell me—I afterwards showed them to Mrs. Eden—she identified them—these are them ( produced).
EMANUEL HARRIS ( policeman, V 184). In consequence of information, I went to a pawnbroker's, at Brentford, and found some boots—I heard Brown say at the station-house that the shirts and boots were her brother's, and that she had pawned them—she said her brother was outside—I went outside, found Smith there, and brought him in—I asked Brown if that was the person she wanted—she said, "Yes"—I asked him where he got the boots—he said they were his own, he had them made at Northampton—I asked him if he sent her to pledge them—he said he did, he gave 14 s. for them—I asked him what stockings he had on—he pulled up his trowsers, and I found these ( produced), which Mrs. Eden identifies—I asked him where he got them—he made no answer—I had seen him about 100 yards from the house about twelve o'clock on the night of the 17th—I do not know whether he was alone or not—he is a stranger to me.
WILLIAM LINEY ( policeman, T 5). The male prisoner told me the two shirts were his, and that he had them made in Hampshire—I said, "Are they marked?"—he said, "Yes"—I asked how—he said he did not know—I got the shirts from Brown.
ABRAHAM REED . I am assistant to Mr. Reed, a pawnbroker, at Brentford. I produce a pair of boots and a shirt, pledged on 18th July, by Brown—the ticket has "Ann Brown" on it ( produced).
JOHN EDEN . These are my boots—I know them by the wear—they were safe that night.
ELIZABETH EDEN re-examined. These shirts and stockings are my husband's property—here is the mark.
Smith's Defence. I found the bundle in a hedge.
Brown's Defence. I met this prisoner; he said he had had no breakfast; I said I had had none, and I pledged the things to get some money; he is not my brother.
SMITH — GUILTY of Stealing. Aged 17.
BROWN — GUILTY of Receiving. Aged 72.
Confined Twelve Months
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18480821-2047]
2047. JOHN SAUNDERS and GEORGE JONES , unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DAWSON conducted the Prosecution.
MARY SYEDS . My husband is a tobacconist, in Rotherhithe Wall. On 15th Aug., Saunders came for half an ounce of tobacco—he paid me with a very new shilling of the Queen's reign—I gave him change for it, and put it into my pocket—I had two old shillings there—a policeman came—I took the three shillings out of my pocket, and at once recognized this one produced, which I had taken of Saunders—the other two were King William's—I gave it to the policeman—this is like the paper of tobacco I sold to Saunders.
ROBERT WILLIAM ANDERSON . I am a fancy soap-maker, of Deptford.
On 15th Aug., Saunders came and asked for half an ounce of bear's greases—he gave me a Victoria shilling—I gave him 10 1/2 d. change, and he left an officer, came in directly, and I gave him the shilling—I had but one other which was Georce the Third's—the officer followed the prisoner—I followed the officer—I fell in with the two prisoners down a turning—they were greasing their hair and laughing—I told Saunders he came to my house just now to buy some bear's grease—he said, "Yes"—I told him he gave me a bad shilling—he said, "Did I"—I said, "You know you did"—he was going to put his hand into his left hand pocket to give me some money, and Jones said, "No, not that pocket"—he put his hand then into his right pocket, and gave me a good shilling—I then asked him for the 10 1/2 d., and he gave it me from the same pocket lie took the good shilling from—I collared him, and called the officer.
JOHN WHITLAMB ( policeman, M 89). On 15th Aug. I went to Mrs. Symm's after Saunders came out—she gave me this shilling—I had seen the prisoners before together, and afterwards I followed them—Saunders went into Mr. Anderson's—Jones was about 100 yards off, apparently watching the door—Saunders came out, and I followed the two prisoners to Hughes' Fields—I saw them rubbing their hands on their heads—I took Saunders.
THOMAS WEST ( policeman, M 249). I went into Mr. Anderson's shop—he gave me this shilling—I followed the prisoners—I afterwards saw Mr. Anderson with them—he called me—I took Jones, and found this tobacco in his pocket.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . I am inspector of coin to the Mint—these shillings are both counterfeit, and cast in the same mould.
SAUNDERS — GUILTY . Aged 22.
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 23.
Confined Four Months

2048. ANN PHILLIPS , for a like offence.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and CLERK conducted the Prosecution.
ARTHUR BLUNT . I live in Regent-street, Lambeth. On 4th Aug. the prisoner came to my shop, and purchased a tea-pot for 6 d.—she gave me half-a-crown—she was on my right—I gave her the change, and she moved to my left side—she said, "This is not right, I purchased the tea-pot for 3 d.—I said it was right, she had purchased it for 6 d. she said she would not have it, she would give me my money again—she gave me two shillings, one was bad—I said I did not give her that bad shilling—she said I did—the shillings I gave her were what I had taken of a customer not five minutes before—they were white—the one she returned was black—several people assembled—a man came from the other side of the way, I had seen him and another man with the prisoner before—he came close to her, and looked over her shoulder, and asked what was the matter—I looked at him, and he went away directly—I believe the white shilling has not been found—I said to the prisoner, "This is bad money, I shall keep it and give you in charge"—she walked into my parlour, and begged very much to be discharged, and said if I would give her the tea-pot she would go about her business—I called a policeman, and gave her in charge.
MARY SHERIDAN . I searched the prisoner at the station—I asked what it was for—she said, "For passing a bad shilling"—I asked if she had got—she said, "No"—I found 1 s. in her bosom, which I marked, and gave to the inspector—she called me very bad names—I said it would be the worse for her, as I should tell the Magistrate—she said, "You have got me now can't be any worse."
JOSEPH WILKINS ( policeman, L 81.) I took the prisoner on 4th Aug. Mr. Blunt gave this shilling to the inspector, and he gave it me, and this other also.
ASSOLAM ARNOLD . This shilling I received from Mr. Blunt, and this other from Mrs. Shann.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . These shillings are both counterfeit.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Four Months.

2049. JAMES MURRAY , for a like offence.
MESSRS. CLERK and SCRIVEN conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL SEYMOUR LOWDEN . I am a baker of Union-place, Clapham. On 26th July the prisoner came into my shop about half-past four o'clock for an Abernethy biscuit—it came to a penny—he put a shilling on the counter—I took it up, and said, "This is bad, have you got another "—he said, "No "—I bent it, and said, "I will get a policeman"—I stepped round the counter, and went to the door—he ran off as fast as he could—Barker went after him—I gave the shilling to the officer.
HENRY BARKER . I was in Mr. Dawson's shop—I saw the prisoner run out—I went after him—I saw him join another man—one gave something to the other—the prisoner went into Mr. Williams' shop—I waited outside—he came out, and Mr. Williams came out—the prisoner joined the other man—they ran off—Mr. Williams and I ran after them, overtook them, and got an officer.
THOMAS WILLIAMS . I am a grocer of Clapham. On 26th July the prisoner came to my shop for half an ounce of tobacco—he gave me a shilling—I gave him 10 1/2 d. change—he went out—I found the shilling was bad—I came to the door—the prisoner was gone—Barker spoke to me—we went in pursuit of him—we saw him and the other man running—they ran 200 or 300 yards; they then stopped—I told the prisoner he gave me a bad shilling—he offered to take it back and give me another—he took out two sixpences and showed me—I gave him in charge—the other man got away—I marked the shilling, and gave it to the officer.
WILLIAM OWEN TUPPNY ( policeman, V 48.) I produce these two shillings one—I received from Mr. Williams, the other from Mr. Dawson—I found two sixpences, a fourpenny-piece, and some halfpence on the prisoner, all good.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . These shillings are both counterfeit.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Four Months.

2052. CHARLES GREY , for a like offence.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and CLERK conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH WRIGHT . My husband keeps a beer-shop in Tooley-street On 6th Aug., the prisoner was there in the evening—I served him with half-a-pint of beer—he paid me a penny—he then asked me for two sixpences for a shilling—I had not two sixpences—I gave him sixpence and sixpenny. worth of halfpence—he gave me a shilling, which I put into the till where there was no other silver—in about five minutes he called fora pint of beer—the girl gave it him—he gave her a shilling, which she gave me—I said it was bad, and returned it to him—he sent me twopence for the beer—he then had half a screw of tobacco, and sent a bad shilling, which I returned—I looked at the first shilling which I had put into the till—it was bad—the prisoner took it out of my hand directly and went away with all the three shillings—the officer has one shilling which I believe to be the one I had from the prisoner first and put into the till.
Prisoner. Did not your husband say he gave the policeman a shilling'? A. No.
WILLIAM WRIGHT . I am the husband of Elizabeth Wright. She called me down stairs on 5th Aug.—I saw the prisoner running out—I followed him, secured him before he got across the street, brought him back, and said. "You have run away with change for a shilling"—he laid down half-a-crown—I gave him 1 s. 6 d., and sent for a policeman—the prisoner went to a public-house and sat behind the door—I gave him in custody—he was brought back to my house and searched, and one bad shilling found on him.
JAMES BARRON . I keep a public-house. On 5th Aug., the prisoner came to my house for a pint of beer—he paid me in coppers, drank his beer, and left—he came back and said he would have a drop of gin—he gave me a shilling—I put it into the till—there was another shilling, a sixpence, and two half-crowns there—it was not put with the other money, but in a corner—the other shilling that was there was battered—the officer came in—I market the shilling the prisoner gave me, and sent it after the officer by Wallis.
Prisoner. Did you not give change for half-a-crown while I was lighting
my pipe? A. No—I did not take out the shilling and pass it to some person to see whether it was good.
MARY WALLINGTON . I gave the shilling which my matser gave me to Hansell, the officer.
DAVID HANSELL ( policeman, M 117). I got this bad shilling from the prisoner, and this other from Wallis—I found on the prisoner 7 s. 2 3/4 d. in good money.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not hear the woman say her husband gave you one of the shillings? A. No.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . These two shillings 3re counterfeit, and cast in the same mould.
( The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that he put down 2 s. 6 d. to bet at a public-house, and mixed it with money belonging to other men, and afterwards found that he must have taken up bad money instead of his own.)
GUILTY .— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18480821-2053]
2053. EDWARD LAWRENCE and THOMAS BARRETT , for a like offence.
MESSRS. SCRIVEN and CLERK conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE SIMPSON . On 1st July, between four and five o'clock in the morning, I was with my mother in the Borough-market—the prisoners came up and bought eighteen gallons of raspberries, and paid 8 s. 3 d. for them—Lawrence gave me four half-crowns, which I gave to my mother—she gave me 2 s. 3 d. change, which I gave to him—Barrett was standing by his side, and had joined in the bargain—I took the raspberries to the opposite side of the market—they were put into one of the prisoner's baskets in a barrow—the prisoners returned in half an hour, and Barrett bought six gallons of raspberries—Barrett paid roe balf-a-crown and a shilling, which I gave to my mother—the prisoners went away—they came back again with a man and woman—the prisoners persuaded them to buy some raspberries, for which they paid three half-crowns—I gave them to my mother—I saw the prisoners together in the Borough-market again on 8th July, and gave them into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Had you seen them in the meantime? A. No—I do not know that they have attended the market four or fire years—I found out that these half-crowns were bad about half an hour after the man and woman left, which was about six o'clock—I had not sold many goods that morning—Keys had not paid me any half-crowns—I did not sell fruit to other persons afterwards—Mr. Fricker has a stand near my mother's.
HANNAH SIMPSON . I am the mother of George Simpson. I was in the Borough-market selling fruit, on 1st July—I saw the prisoners come for eighteen gallons of raspberries—I saw Lawrence pay my son four half-crowns—he gave them to me, I put them in my pocket—the prisoners went away they returned, and Barrett bought some raspberries which came to 3 s. 6 d.—I saw him pay a half-crown and a shilling to my son—he brought it to me—I put it in the same pocket with the others—no one had been to purchase fruit in the meantime—the prisoners went away, and came back with a man and woman—they bought some raspberries—I cannot tell what money my son gave me, but I put it into my pocket—no one had been to deal in the meantime—I had no money in my pocket but what I had from my son—after I had sold the last lot, something was said, and I took eight half-crowns and two shillings out of my pocket.
Crass-examined. Q. You do not know which were the three nor which
were the five? A. No, but they were all bad—I marked them and gave them to the beadle about two minutes after the four people were gone—I had no other half-crown in my pocket—the prisoners did not pay six shilling and five sixpences—I had not received any money from Keys—I never saw the prisoners before.
HENRY CHANNING . I am beadle of the Borough-market. I produce the eight half-crowns and two shillings which Mrs. Simpson gave me.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know these men? A. I have seen them about the market.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . These eight half-crowns are all counterfeit—these two shillings are counterfeit, and cast in the same mould—three of these half-crowns have been cast in one mould, and five in another.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
LAWRENCE — GUILTY . Aged 22.
BARRETT — GUILTY . Aged 24.
Confined Two Months.

2054. JAMES STEVENS , stealing a show-case and pictures, value 22 l.; the goods of Antoine Claudet; to which he pleaded
